Explicit error bounds and guaranteed convergence of the Koopman-Hill projection stability method for linear time-periodic dynamics
Fabia Bayer, Remco I. Leine

Abstract
The Koopman-Hill projection method offers an efficient approach for stability analysis of linear time-periodic systems, and thereby also for the Floquet stability analysis of periodic solutions of nonlinear systems. However, its accuracy has previously been supported only by numerical evidence, lacking rigorous theoretical guarantees. This paper presents the first explicit error bound for the truncation error of the Koopman-Hill projection method, establishing a solid theoretical foundation for its application. The bound applies to linear time-periodic systems whose Fourier coefficients decay exponentially with a sufficient rate, and is derived using constructive series expansions. The bound quantifies the difference between the true and approximated fundamental solution matrices, clarifies conditions for guaranteed convergence, and enables conservative but reliable inference of Floquet multipliers and stability properties. Additionally, the same methodology applied to a subharmonic formulation demonstrates improved convergence rates of the latter. Numerical examples, including the Mathieu equation and the Duffing oscillator, illustrate the practical relevance of the bound and underscore its importance as the first rigorous theoretical justification for the Koopman-Hill projection method.



1
Introduction

The Koopman-Hill projection method was introduced in
[
1
]
to approximate the fundamental solution matrix of linear time-periodic systems. However, the justification for the method in prior work, which is based on the Koopman framework, does not provide any insight into the magnitude of the error incurred by truncation. In principle, this error could become very large and render the method useless. Prior works reported only numerical evidence for the accuracy of the method
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
. The central contribution of the present work is to strengthen the theoretical foundation of the Koopman-Hill method by providing a closed-form bound for the truncation error for a certain class of linear time-periodic systems.

Floquet theory is the mathematical field concerning the evolution and the local stability analysis of linear time-periodic (LTP) ordinary differential equations (ODEs) of the form

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{y}}(t)=\mathbf{J}(t)\mathbf{y}(t)\;,

(1)

where the system matrix
\mathbf{J}(t)=\mathbf{J}(t+T)\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is periodic with period
T
.
This field of study, pioneered by Gaston Floquet in the 1880s
[
4
]
, remains of prime interest in various fields of engineering and applied mathematics. While parametrically excited mechanical systems such as simulation models for interacting gears
[
5
]
, MEMS devices
[
6
]
, or helicopter ground resonance problems
[
7
]
can intrinsically showcase LTP dynamics, arguably the largest field of application of Floquet theory is the study of stability of periodic orbits or periodic solutions of nonlinear dynamical systems. Periodic solutions arise naturally in many applications, such as structural dynamics
[
8
]
and electrical circuits
[
9
]
. While only stable periodic orbits can be attained in practical experiments, unstable periodic solutions characterize the global behavior of a system, and knowledge of them can be critical to safe operation
[
10
]
.
If a nonlinear ordinary differential equation with a periodic orbit is linearized around that periodic orbit, the resulting dynamical system is LTP and of the form of Equation (
1
). Due to the Hartman-Grobman theorem, the stability properties of the periodic solution are equivalent to those of the trivial solution of Equation (
1
) in the hyperbolic case
[
11
]
.

In contrast to linear time-invariant systems, closed-form solutions for LTP systems with more than one state are usually not available. The stability of the LTP system in Equation (
1
) is not immediately determined by the spectrum of the system matrix
\mathbf{J}(t)
, so sophisticated numerical methods are required. Methods for numerical stability analysis of LTP systems or, equivalently, hyperbolic periodic orbits of nonlinear ODEs, can classically be categorized into two families, with their corresponding operations taking place in the time domain or in the frequency domain, respectively.

The time domain approaches rely on numerical integration of the ODE (
1
) over one period to obtain the system’s monodromy matrix, whose eigenvalues govern the stability. While effective, these methods can be computationally expensive, especially for systems with long periods or many states. The approximation error that these approaches incur is determined by the chosen numerical integration scheme
[
12
,
13
]
. Global error bounds depending on the local error for fixed or variable-step integration methods are available for Lipschitz continuous dynamics
[
13
]
, but are not often explicitly computed in practice. Integration schemes that specifically measure the global error are computationally expensive
[
14
]
.

Frequency domain methods are based on Fourier series representations of the LTP system of Equation (
1
) and its solutions. This leads to the formulation of an eigenvalue problem involving the bi-infinite Hill matrix, which captures the system’s characteristics in the frequency domain
[
15
,
16
]
. This approach is especially appealing for stability analysis of periodic solutions computed numerically by the harmonic balance method (HBM), where a truncated variant of this Hill matrix is immediately available as a byproduct of HBM. The spectrum of the truncated matrix consists of physically meaningful Floquet exponents, as well as spurious eigenvalues without physical meaning due to truncation effects.

Sorting criteria
[
17
,
16
,
18
]
are commonly used to identify the physically meaningful eigenvalues. The imaginary-part-based sorting criterion proposed by Zhou et al.
[
19
]
is guaranteed to converge as the truncation order tends to infinity, but no bounds are provided for any finite truncation order
N
.
Other eigenvalue sorting methods can exhibit better convergence rates in some numerical examples
[
20
,
21
]
, but come with no convergence guarantee whatsoever. In addition, in the vicinity of period-doubling bifurcations, the non-uniqueness of Floquet exponents poses a challenge for all sorting-based methods
[
22
]
.

The Koopman-Hill method, introduced by the authors in 2023
[
1
]
, offers a novel approach combining aspects of both frequency and time domains. By reinterpreting the truncated Hill matrix as the system matrix of a linear time-
in
variant differential equation, the fundamental solution matrix of the original system, Equation (
1
), can be obtained using a single matrix exponential involving the Hill matrix, bypassing direct numerical integration. Previous numerical studies have shown that this method provides accurate approximations of the monodromy matrix, with the approximation error decreasing as the truncation order of the Hill matrix increases
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
23
]
.

Despite the promising numerical performance, the previously available justification for the Koopman-Hill method based on the Koopman framework
[
1
]
, recounted and discussed in Section
2.2
, has lacked a rigorous error analysis. The central contribution of the present work is to strengthen the Koopman-Hill method by providing a closed-form error bound for the difference between the Koopman-Hill approximation and the fundamental solution matrix. The approach taken here does not rely on methodology from the Koopman framework. Rather, the idea is to find exact series expressions for the considered matrices, compare them term by term, and bound the difference. The considered series expression is novel to the authors’ knowledge. The resulting bound decays exponentially with the truncation order of the Hill matrix. It depends only on two scalar parameters which govern the decay behavior of the Fourier coefficients of the original LTP dynamics and is applicable whenever these Fourier coefficients decay exponentially at a sufficient rate.

Previous works of the authors
[
1
,
2
]
observed that a modified formulation of the Koopman-Hill method that includes additional, subharmonic frequencies can lead to a significant improvement in the approximation accuracy.
However, these works did not provide an explanation for this improved performance. Using the formalism developed in this paper, we provide also an error bound for the subharmonic formulation. Compared to the error bound of the direct formulation, this error bound decays at twice the rate, indicating improved accuracy.

The structure of this paper is as follows.
Section
2
provides the necessary theoretical background on Floquet theory and summarizes the previous motivation for the Koopman-Hill projection method based on the Koopman framework. Section
3
introduces our main results. After stating the involved series expressions, we derive the closed-form error bound in Section
3
. The subharmonic formulation is introduced and bounded subsequently in Section
4
. The proofs for the series expressions which were stated in Section
3
are carried out in Section
5
. Section
6
illustrates the theoretical findings with three numerical examples. Finally, Section
7
concludes the paper with a summary and discussion of the main results and an outlook on future research.

2
Theoretical background and notation

This section provides the necessary background for the developments to follow. First, we revisit Floquet theory. Next, the arguments of
[
1
]
, justifying the Koopman-Hill projection method for computing the fundamental solution matrix of Equation (
1
), are summarized. Further, the multi-index notation that is used throughout the remainder of this paper is introduced.

2.1
Floquet Theory

This section revisits some well-known facts from Floquet theory that are the basis for the developments of the following sections. For a more comprehensive treatment of the concepts of this section, the interested reader is referred to
[
24
,
25
,
11
]
. Floquet theory is concerned with the study of linear time-periodic (LTP) dynamical systems given by Equation (
1
). We restrict the class of considered LTP systems to ones where the system matrix has a pointwise convergent Fourier series

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}(t)=\mathbf{J}(t+T)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{J}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\omega t}

(2)

with the complex-valued Fourier coefficient matrices
\dots,\mathbf{J}_{-1},\mathbf{J}_{0},\mathbf{J}_{1},\dots\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
. A sufficient criterion for the existence of this Fourier series is that
\mathbf{J}(t)
is piecewise continuous with piecewise continuous derivative
[
26
]
, which is the case in many engineering applications.

Due to the linearity of Equation (
1
), any initial condition
\mathbf{y}(t_{0})
is mapped to its solution
\mathbf{y}(t)=\mathbf{\Phi}(t,t_{0})\mathbf{y}(t_{0})
by the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t,t_{0})
, which is the unique solution of the matrix initial value problem

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{\Phi}}(t,t_{0})=\mathbf{J}(t)\mathbf{\Phi}(t,t_{0})

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(t_{0},t_{0})=\mathbf{I}\;.

(3)

In the literature, any full-rank
n\times n
matrix that satisfies
\dot{\mathbf{\Phi}}=\mathbf{J}\mathbf{\Phi}
is often referred to as a fundamental solution matrix, regardless of its specific initial condition. The solution of (
3
), i.e., the specific fundamental solution matrix with the identity initial condition, is then termed the
principal
fundamental solution matrix
[
24
]
. However, for brevity, we will refer to the unique solution of Equation (
3
) simply as the fundamental solution matrix. Setting
t_{0}=0
without loss of generality, we denote the fundamental solution matrix as
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)=\mathbf{\Phi}(t,0)
.

Floquet’s theorem
[
24
]
states that the fundamental solution matrix can be expressed as

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(t)=\mathbf{P}(t)\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{Q}t}\;,

(4)

where
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
is a constant matrix and
\mathbf{P}(t)=\mathbf{P}(t+T)
is a
T
-periodic complex-valued matrix with
\mathbf{P}(0)=\mathbf{I}
. The long-term behavior of the solution is solely determined by the eigenvalues
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n}
of
\mathbf{Q}
, which are referred to as the Floquet exponents. If all Floquet exponents have a negative real part, then the equilibrium of the system given by Equation (
1
) is asymptotically stable. If the LTP system (
1
) was obtained by linearization of a nonlinear ODE around a periodic solution, then the Floquet exponents also govern the stability of that periodic solution.

The fundamental solution matrix evaluated after one period,
\mathbf{\Phi}_{T}:=\mathbf{\Phi}(T)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{Q}T}
, is called the monodromy matrix. It is of special interest as it provides a discrete mapping of states from one period to the next. If all eigenvalues of the monodromy matrix are inside the unit circle, the solution will always decay from one period to the next, indicating asymptotic stability of the equilibrium at zero. In contrast, if at least one eigenvalue is outside the unit circle, the solution in the corresponding eigendirection will grow from one period to the next, indicating instability. The eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
of the monodromy matrix are called Floquet multipliers and it can be easily seen from Floquet’s theorem, Equation (
4
), that they are related to the Floquet exponents by
\lambda_{i}=\mathrm{e}^{\alpha_{i}T}
.

Since the monodromy matrix is usually not available in closed form, numerical methods are needed to approximate the Floquet multipliers. An overview over various methods to obtain the monodromy matrix by numerical integration of Equation (
3
) is given in
[
12
]
. These numerical integration methods will not be treated further in this work. Alternatively, the periodic nature of the differential equation can be exploited to determine the stability based on the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{J}
. These so-called Hill methods rely on the Hill matrix, which is constituted of by the Fourier coefficients of the system matrix
\mathbf{J}(t)
. The Hill methods classically consider the full spectral decomposition of the Hill matrix to assert stability
[
12
,
16
]
. The following section introduces the Koopman-Hill projection method
[
1
]
, which combines aspects of the classical Hill method with (closed-form) time-domain integration over a period.

2.2
The Koopman-Hill projection method motivated through the Koopman framework

Homogeneous linear time-invariant (LTI) systems have a closed-form solution given by the matrix exponential and are therefore straightforward to solve, in contrast to LTP systems such as Equation (
1
). The Koopman-Hill projection method, introduced in
[
1
]
, is based on the idea of approximating the nonautonomous dynamics of Equation (
1
) with an LTI system in a higher-dimensional state space. This approach is motivated by the Koopman framework, which represents nonlinear autonomous systems as linear autonomous systems of larger, potentially infinite, dimension. The following exposition summarizes the arguments from
[
1
]
that motivate the use of a matrix exponential to approximate the fundamental solution matrix of the LTP system (
1
).

The Koopman operator was originally developed to extract global information from nonlinear autonomous dynamics via its spectral decomposition, known as Koopman eigenfunctions
[
27
,
28
,
29
]
. More recently, the Koopman operator has been applied in data-driven modeling to construct linear systems that capture nonlinear behavior
[
30
,
31
,
32
]
. For the purposes of the Koopman-Hill projection, only the matrix representation of the Koopman generator is required. Readers interested in further concepts are referred to the extensive literature on the Koopman operator, such as
[
29
]
and references therein.

Consider a nonlinear autonomous dynamical system

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})

(5)

in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, with the flow map
\mathbf{x}(t)=\varphi(t,\mathbf{x}(0))
. Let
\mathcal{F}
denote a Banach space of observable functions
g:\mathbb{R}^{n}\rightarrow\mathbb{C}
. The semigroup of Koopman operators
\mathcal{K}^{t}:g\mapsto g\circ\varphi(t,\cdot)
describes the evolution of observables along system trajectories
[
29
]
. Under suitable regularity conditions (see
[
33
]
), this semigroup is strongly continuous and generated by the infinitesimal Koopman generator

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\lim_{t\rightarrow 0}\frac{\mathcal{K}^{t}-\mathbf{I}}{t}\;,

(6)

which acts as the Lie derivative of the observable along the flow:

\displaystyle(\mathcal{L}g)(\mathbf{x})=\dot{g}(\mathbf{x})=\frac{\partial g}{\partial\mathbf{x}}\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})\;.

(7)

For example, the derivative of a Lyapunov function
V
along system trajectories is given by
\dot{V}=\mathcal{L}V
. The infinitesimal Koopman generator
\mathcal{L}
is linear on the space of observables, even though the original dynamics is nonlinear.

To apply the Koopman framework to the linear time-periodic system (
1
), it is necessary to reformulate the nonautonomous ODE as an autonomous system. This is achieved by introducing an extended state space with dynamics

\displaystyle\dot{t}

\displaystyle=1

(8a)

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{J}(t)\mathbf{y}\;,

(8b)

where the extended state is
\mathbf{x}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}=(t,\mathbf{y}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}})
. As function space
\mathcal{F}
, consider specifically the span of the complex-valued basis functions

\displaystyle g_{k,l}(t,\mathbf{y})=y_{l}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\omega t}

(9)

for
k\in\mathbb{Z}
and
l\in\left\{1,\dots,n\right\}
, equipped with the
L_{2}
norm.

Define the collection
\mathbf{g}_{k}:=(y_{1}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\omega t},\dots,y_{n}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\omega t})^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}
of all
n
states at one specific frequency as the
k
-th block of basis functions. Using the Fourier series expansion of
\mathbf{J}(t)
from Equation (
2
), the derivative of the
k
-th block is

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{g}}_{k}(t,\mathbf{y})

\displaystyle=\left(-\mathrm{i}k\omega\mathbf{I}+\sum_{l\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{J}_{l}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}l\omega t}\right)\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}k\omega t}\mathbf{y}=-\mathrm{i}k\omega\mathbf{g}_{k}+\sum_{\tilde{l}\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{J}_{k-\tilde{l}}\,\mathbf{g}_{\tilde{l}}\;,

(10)

where the Fourier series expression of Equation (
2
) for
\mathbf{J}(t)
is used.

Stacking all blocks of basis functions by decreasing frequency yields the bi-infinite vector

\displaystyle\mathbf{g}(t,\mathbf{y})=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\vdots\\
\mathbf{y}\mathrm{e}^{2\mathrm{i}\omega t}\\
\mathbf{y}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega t}\\
\mathbf{y}\\
\mathbf{y}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\\
\mathbf{y}\mathrm{e}^{-2\mathrm{i}\omega t}\\
\vdots\end{array}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\mathbf{D}_{\infty}\omega t}\mathbf{W}_{\infty}\mathbf{y}\;.

Here,
\mathbf{D}_{\infty}={\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}}(...,-2\mathbf{I},-\mathbf{I},\mathbf{0},\mathbf{I},2\mathbf{I},\dots)
is a diagonal bi-infinite matrix whose diagonal entries are the integers in ascending order, each repeated
n
times. The operator
\mathbf{W}_{\infty}=(\dots,\mathbf{I},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{I},\dots)^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}
is a bi-infinite stack of
n\times n
identity matrices, stacking the vector
\mathbf{y}
repeatedly. To describe the evolution of the observables for an arbitrary initial condition
\mathbf{y}_{0}
at
t_{0}=0
, the action of the Koopman operator on
\mathbf{g}
can be written as

\displaystyle\left(\mathcal{K}^{t}\mathbf{g}\right)(0,\mathbf{y}_{0})=\mathbf{g}(t,\mathbf{y}(t))=\left(\begin{array}[]{r}\vdots\\
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega t}\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\vdots\end{array}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}_{\infty}t}\underbrace{\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\vdots\\
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\\
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\\
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\\
\vdots\end{array}\right)}_{=:\mathbf{Q}_{\infty}(t)}\mathbf{y}_{0}\;.

The matrix
\mathbf{Q}_{\infty}(t)
collects a bi-infinite stack of fundamental solution matrices, each of which evolves the initial condition pertaining to a certain frequency.
From Equation (
10
), the Lie derivative of the bi-infinite basis vector
\mathbf{g}
is

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{g}}=\begin{pmatrix}\ddots&\\
\dots&\mathbf{J}_{0}+\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{J}_{-1}&\mathbf{J}_{-2}&\dots\\
\dots&\mathbf{J}_{1}&\mathbf{J}_{0}&\mathbf{J}_{-1}&\dots\\
\dots&\mathbf{J}_{2}&\mathbf{J}_{1}&\mathbf{J}_{0}-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{I}&\dots\\
&&&&\ddots\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{g}=:\mathbf{H}_{\infty}\mathbf{g}\;,

(28)

which is linear in
\mathbf{g}
. The bi-infinite block matrix
\mathbf{H}_{\infty}
of the above derivative expression, constructed from the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{J}(t)
, is well-known in the study of LTP systems as the bi-infinite Hill matrix
[
16
,
15
]
. Its point spectrum consists of the Floquet exponents, while its continuous and residual spectra are either empty or contain only
\left\{\pm\infty\right\}

[
19
]
. The operator
\mathbf{H}_{\infty}
is unbounded in
\ell_{2}
due to the
\mathrm{i}\omega
terms on the diagonal, a common feature of Koopman lifts not expressed in a finite basis of eigenfunctions
[
34
,
32
]
.

Consider a bi-infinite initial condition
\mathbf{z}_{0}\in\ell_{\infty}
that is consistent with
\mathbf{g}
, meaning that there exist
t_{0}
and
\mathbf{y}_{0}
such that
\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{g}(t_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})
. Substituting
\mathbf{g}
with
\mathbf{z}\in\ell_{\infty}
in Equation (
28
) yields the Cauchy problem

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{z}}=\mathbf{H}_{\infty}\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}(t_{0})=\mathbf{z}_{0}=\mathbf{g}(t_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})\;.

(29)

However, for every consistent initial condition
\mathbf{z}_{0}\neq\mathbf{0}
one has
\left\|\mathbf{H}_{\infty}\mathbf{z}_{0}\right\|_{\infty}=\infty
, so the Cauchy problem (
29
) is ill-posed.
This issue is typically addressed in the Koopman framework by projecting the Koopman generator onto a finite-dimensional subspace, which necessarily introduces truncation errors
[
35
]
. In general cases, this truncation may completely change the problem at hand and there is no guarantee that the result is accurate. However, there exists a multitude of examples in literature involving Dynamic Mode Decomposition that show good accuracy despite the truncation, e.g.
[
34
]
.

Specifically for the linear time-periodic case discussed here, the truncation entails that only the
2N+1
central observable blocks
\mathbf{g}_{-N},\dots,\mathbf{g}_{N}
are retained, and all other blocks in Equation (
10
) are discarded. The bi-infinite Hill matrix
\mathbf{H}_{\infty}
is thus replaced by its finite-dimensional truncation
\mathbf{H}\in\mathbb{C}^{n(2N+1)\times n(2N+1)}
, given by its
(2N+1)
central row and column blocks. For instance, for
N=2
, the truncated Hill matrix is

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}=\left(\begin{array}[]{lllll}\hbox to20.4pt{\vbox to20.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.20001pt\lower-12.35278pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.21527pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -7.22 -2.98)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-6.5625pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -9.08 -2.98)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}-\hbox to22.76pt{\vbox to20.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 11.3817pt\lower-10.20001pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -15.47 -13.84 h 30.94 v 27.67 h -30.94 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-9.1817pt}{-3.43056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -12.7 -4.75)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}\right)\;,

where Fourier coefficient matrices
\mathbf{J}_{k}
up to
k=\pm 2N=\pm 4
appear. The background boxes in Equation (
2.2
) indicate repeated blocks of
\mathbf{H}
and highlight its block-Toeplitz-like structure.
The consistently initialized truncated initial value problem, involving the truncated matrix
\mathbf{H}
instead of the bi-infinite operator
\mathbf{H}_{\infty}
, thus becomes

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{z}}=\mathbf{H}\mathbf{z}

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}(t_{0})=[\mathbf{g}_{-N}(t_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}},\dots,\mathbf{g}_{N}(t_{0},\mathbf{y}_{0})^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}]^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}\;.

(35)

By Equation (
9
), consistent truncated initial conditions are linear in
\mathbf{y}_{0}
and given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}(t_{0})=\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t_{0}}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{y}_{0}=:\mathbf{z}_{0}\;,

(36)

where
\mathbf{W}
is a vertical stack of
2N+1
identity matrices and
\mathbf{D}={\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}}\left(-N,\dots,N\right)\otimes\mathbf{I}
is a diagonal matrix that incorporates the initial time. They are the finite truncations of
\mathbf{W}_{\infty}
and
\mathbf{D}_{\infty}
, respectively. If
t_{0}=0
, then
\mathbf{z}(0)=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{y}_{0}
. For the case
N=2
, the
\mathbf{W}
and
\mathbf{D}
matrices associated with
\mathbf{H}
from Equation (
2.2
) are

\displaystyle\mathbf{D}=\left(\begin{array}[]{lllll}-2\mathbf{I}\\
&-\mathbf{I}\\
&&\mathbf{0}\\
&&&\mathbf{I}\\
&&&&2\mathbf{I}\end{array}\right)\;,

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}=\left(\begin{array}[]{l}\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{I}\\
\mathbf{I}\end{array}\right)\;,

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}(0)=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\mathbf{y}_{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}\;.

Unlike the bi-infinite case, Equation (
35
) is a well-defined finite linear homogeneous initial value problem with
n(2N+1)
states. Its solution at time
t
is given by the matrix exponential

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}(t)=\exp(\mathbf{H}(t-t_{0}))\,\mathbf{W}\mathbf{y}_{0}\;.

(47)

In the absence of errors due to truncation, the evolution of
\mathbf{z}(t)
generated by the truncated infinitesimal Koopman generator would coincide with the evolution of the
2N+1
innermost entries of
\mathbf{g}
under the Koopman operator, according to Equation (
2.2
). In practice, one hopes that this relationship is approximately preserved for sufficiently large
N

[
32
]
. This idea is visualized in the left half of Figure
1
: Evolving the state using the LTP system of Equation (
1
) and applying the observable function afterwards should yield approximately the same results as applying the observable function to the initial state and evolving the result under the truncated lifted LTI dynamics of Equation (
35
). Previous numerical studies have shown that, indeed, at least the difference between the centermost blocks of
\mathbf{z}(t)
and
\mathbf{g}(t,\mathbf{y}(t))
does tend to zero as the truncation order
N
increases
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
.

In the right half of Figure
1
, both the vectors
\mathbf{g}(t,\mathbf{y}(t))
in the upper row and
\mathbf{z}(t)
in the lower row are pre-multiplied by
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\mathbf{D}\omega t}
. This operation does not alter the truncation error, but it removes the exponential scaling factor from each block of
\mathbf{g}
, cf. Equation (
2.2
). Each block of the resulting matrix contains
\mathbf{y}(t)
(upper row) or an approximation of
\mathbf{y}(t)
(lower row). Hereby, all the individual approximations need not be equal and may have differing truncation errors.

Figure 1
:
Schematic of the Koopman-Hill projection method. The top row illustrates the evolution of
\mathbf{y}_{0}
under the original LTP dynamics and subsequent application of
\mathbf{g}
to the evolved state, while the bottom row shows the evolution using the truncated lifted LTI dynamics after application of
\mathbf{g}
to the initial condition. If the truncation error is small, the bottom row approximates the top row.

The final step is to generalize from the evolution of a single initial condition
\mathbf{y}_{0}
to the fundamental solution matrix. This generalization is straightforward. The fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
is defined as the solution of Equation (
3
) with the initial condition
\mathbf{y}(0)=\mathbf{I}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{y}_{0}
can be replaced by
\mathbf{I}
, or simply omitted from all equations in Figure
1
. The resulting matrix

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(t):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{-N}(t)\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{Q}_{N}(t)\end{pmatrix}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}

(48)

consists of
2N+1
blocks
\mathbf{Q}_{j}(t)
for
j=-N,\dots,N
, each of size
n\times n
. When the truncation error is small and the top and bottom rows of Figure
1
approximate each other,
\mathbf{Q}(t)
is an approximation of the centermost blocks of
\mathbf{Q}_{\infty}
, defined in Equation (
2.2
). Hence, each
n\times n
block of
\mathbf{Q}(t)
provides an approximation of the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
with yet-undetermined approximation error. In particular, the central block
\mathbf{Q}_{0}(t)
can be extracted by matrix multiplication, yielding the following approximation for the monodromy matrix:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}_{T}

\displaystyle\approx\mathbf{C}\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}T}\,\mathbf{W}

(49a)

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{0}&\dots&\mathbf{0}&\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{0}&\dots&\mathbf{0}\end{pmatrix}

(49b)

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{I}&\dots&\mathbf{I}\end{pmatrix}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}\;.

(49c)

This formulation was introduced in
[
1
]
as the Koopman-Hill projection method. We emphasize that, in prior work, only numerical evidence was presented for the accuracy of this method and the truncation error has not been theoretically quantified. The present work addresses this gap by providing a closed-form bound for the truncation error for a certain class of linear time-periodic systems.

2.3
Multi-index notation

For the sake of brevity, we introduce here two different types of index notation, which are used throughout this work.
The natural numbers
\mathbb{N}
always include zero.
We denote
1
-norms by
\left|.\right|
(with one vertical bar), even for tuples with more than one entry.
By
\left\|.\right\|
(with two vertical bars) we denote an arbitrary vector
p
-norm and its induced matrix norm.

Definition 1

(Multi-index)
.

A tuple
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
of
m
nonnegative integers is called
multi-index (in the classical sense)
. The multi-index has a 1-norm

\displaystyle\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|:=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\alpha_{k}

(50)

given by the sum of its entries. For a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{C}^{m}
, we denote by

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{\bm{\alpha}}:=\prod_{k=1}^{m}x_{k}^{\alpha_{k}}

(51)

monomials in
\mathbf{x}
of degree
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|
. We define
0^{0}:=1
to avoid restricting the values that
\mathbf{x}
may admit.

Example 2.1
.

The multi-index
\bm{\alpha}=[2,0,1,0]
has norm
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|
=
3
and
\mathbf{x}^{\bm{\alpha}}=x_{1}^{2}x_{3}
for all
\mathbf{x}=[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]\in\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

Definition 2

(Products of Fourier coefficient matrices)
.

A tuple
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
of
m
integers is called an
integer index tuple
. It has a 1-norm

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{p}\right|:=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\left|p_{k}\right|\;,

(52)

which is generally not equal to the sum of its entries. Given a bi-infinite sequence of Fourier coefficient matrices
\left(\mathbf{J}_{k}\right)_{k\in\mathbb{Z}}
, any integer index tuple
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
uniquely describes an (ordered)
product of
m
Fourier coefficient matrices

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}:=\prod_{k=1}^{m}\mathbf{J}_{p_{k}}=\mathbf{J}_{p_{1}}\mathbf{J}_{p_{2}}\dots\mathbf{J}_{p_{m}}\;.

(53)

The ordering of the entries of
\mathbf{p}
is important as the matrices
\mathbf{J}_{k}
generally do not commute.

Example 2.2
.

Consider as an example the integer index tuple
\mathbf{p}=[-3,0,1,1]
, which defines uniquely the product of Fourier coefficients

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{[-3,0,1,1]}=\mathbf{J}_{-3}\,\mathbf{J}_{0}\,\mathbf{J}_{1}\,\mathbf{J}_{1}=\mathbf{J}_{-3}\,\mathbf{J}_{0}\,\mathbf{J}_{1}^{2}\;.

As
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
is a subset of
\mathbb{C}^{m}
, integer index tuples can be exponentiated by multi-indices using Equation (
51
). With
\bm{\alpha}=[2,0,1,0]
and
\mathbf{p}=[-3,0,1,1]
as before, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}^{\bm{\alpha}}=[-3,0,1,1]^{[2,0,1,0]}=(-3)^{2}\cdot 0^{0}\cdot 1^{1}\cdot 1^{0}=9

and even

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}^{\bm{\alpha}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}=9\,\mathbf{J}_{-3}\,\mathbf{J}_{0}\,\mathbf{J}_{1}^{2}\;.

Example 2.3
.

For an integer index tuple
\mathbf{p}=[p_{1},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
and a multi-index
\bm{\alpha}=[\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{m}]\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
, a specific multi-index expression that will become important later is

\displaystyle[p_{1},p_{1}+p_{2},\dots,p_{1}+\dots+p_{m}]^{\bm{\alpha}}=\prod_{k=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{l=1}^{k}p_{l}\right)^{\alpha_{k}}=p_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}(p_{1}+p_{2})^{\alpha_{2}}\dots(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})^{\alpha_{m}}\;,

(54)

consisting of a product of
m
factors, where the
k
-th factor is given by the sum of the first
k
entries of the integer index tuple, exponentiated by
\alpha_{k}
.

3
Main result: Error bound

Section
2.2
motivated that the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(t)
, defined in Equation (
48
) as
\mathbf{Q}(t):=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}
with
\mathbf{H}
being the truncated Hill matrix, provides approximations of the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
. More specifically, the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(t)
consists of
2N+1
blocks
\mathbf{Q}_{j}(t)
, for
j=-N,\dots,N
. Each block
\mathbf{Q}_{j}(t)
of size
n\times n
is expected to approximate the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
.
However, the motivation in Section
2.2
, based on the Koopman framework, does not provide any insight into the magnitude of the error incurred by truncation. In principle, this error could become very large and render the method useless. Thus, the Koopman-based motivation for Equation (
49
) is not sufficient to prove that
\mathbf{Q}_{j}(t)\approx\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
is indeed the case.

The central contribution of the present work is to strengthen the Koopman-Hill projection method, i.e., justify that Equation (
49
) approximates the fundamental solution matrix, by providing a closed-form bound for the difference between the central block
\mathbf{Q}_{0}(t)
of Equation (
48
) and the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
. The approach taken here does not rely on methodology from the Koopman framework. Rather, the idea is to find exact series expressions for the considered matrices
\mathbf{Q}_{0}(t)
and
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
, compare them term by term, and bound the difference. For both the true fundamental solution matrix and the Koopman-Hill approximation, it is natural to consider Taylor series expansions around the initial time
t_{0}=0
.

For the Taylor series of
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
, the values of the true fundamental solution and its first derivative at
t_{0}=0
are immediately known from the matrix initial value problem of Equation (
3
). Further derivatives at
0
can be found inductively by the product rule as polynomials of derivatives of
\mathbf{J}(t)
, evaluated at zero. With
\mathbf{J}(t)
and all its derivatives expressed as Fourier series, a combined Taylor-Fourier series expression for
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
, characterized by products of the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{J}(t)
, can be established. This constructive process is described in more detail in
A
. The absolute convergence of the resulting series expression can be ensured by the following assumption, which in some sense characterizes the dynamical system under consideration.

Assumption 1
.

There are constants
a,b>0
and a matrix
p
-norm
\left\|\cdot\right\|
such that the Fourier coefficient matrices of
\mathbf{J}(t)
in Equation (
1
) decay exponentially with

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{J}_{k}\right\|\leq a\mathrm{e}^{-b\left|k\right|}

\displaystyle\text{for all\penalty\ }k\in\mathbb{Z}\;.

(55)

By the Paley-Wiener theorem, Assumption
1
holds if
\mathbf{J}(t)
is analytic
[
36
, Lemma 5.6]
.
In the context of stability of periodic solutions of nonlinear dynamics, analyticity of a nonlinear differential equation in all its arguments is sufficient to guarantee analyticity of its periodic solution and thus analyticity of the linearization
\mathbf{J}(t)

[
24
, Thm. 4.1]
.
In the special case where the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{J}(t)
have finite support, Assumption
1
is satisfied for any
b>0
with
a=\max_{k}\left\|\mathbf{J}_{k}\right\|\mathrm{e}^{b\left|k\right|}
.

Our first theorem establishes the Taylor-Fourier series for the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
.

Theorem 1

(Series formulation of
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
)
.

If the Fourier coefficient matrices of
\mathbf{J}(t)
in Equation (
1
) fulfill Assumption
1
, then the fundamental solution matrix is given by the absolutely convergent series

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}

(56)

with the scalar, complex-valued factor

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t):=\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\frac{t^{m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{(m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}\left[p_{1},p_{1}+p_{2},\dots,p_{1}+\dots+p_{m}\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}

(57)

that is independent of the specific dynamical system and bounded by

\displaystyle\left|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right|\leq\frac{\left|t\right|^{m}}{m!}\;.

(58)

Theorem
1
is proven in Section
5.2
. The fundamental solution matrix is expressed here in terms of ordered monomials
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
of Fourier coefficients of the system matrix
\mathbf{J}(t)
, with each such monomial multiplied by a scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
that only depends on the time
t
and the degree
\mathbf{p}
of the monomial, and not on the considered system. Figure
2
presents the values of the scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
for three different values of
\mathbf{p}
, exemplifying three central properties:
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
is complex-valued, of oscillatory nature, and polynomially bounded. For different values of
\mathbf{p}
, the values of
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
can be of vastly differing magnitude.

(a)

\mathbf{p}=[-1,-2,3]

(b)

\mathbf{p}=[1,-1,0,1,-1,1]

(c)

\mathbf{p}=[4,-1]

Figure 2
:
Real part (black), imaginary part (gray), and polynomial upper bound (dashed) of the scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
over three periods for three exemplary values of
\mathbf{p}
. Data was computed using Lemma
9
and numerical quadrature with trapezoidal rule.

The proof of Theorem
1
in Section
5.2
relies on derivative properties of the scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
to show that the series in Equation (
56
) uniquely solves the matrix initial value problem (
3
) and must thus be the fundamental solution matrix. However, this approach is not constructive and does not explain why the series is of its specific structure. For additional insight,
A
sketches an alternative, more illustrative derivation of Equations (
56
) and (
57
) based on the Taylor expansion of
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
and the Fourier series of
\mathbf{J}(t)
.

Similarly, a series expression for the blocks of the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(t)
, which are expected to approximate the fundamental solution matrix according to Figure
1
and Equation (
2.2
), can be derived by expressing Equation (
48
) as a Taylor series. Since the matrix
\mathbf{H}
consists of the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{J}(t)
(cf. Equation (
28
)), the summands of the resulting Taylor series are again characterized by polynomials of these Fourier coefficients.

Theorem 2

(Series expression for the Koopman-Hill approximations
\mathbf{Q}(t)
)
.

For any index
j\in\left\{-N,\dots,N\right\}
, the
j
-th
n\times n
-block of the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(t)
defined in Equation (
48
) is given by the series

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}_{j}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;,

(59)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}=\left\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:\left|j-\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}\right|\leq N\text{\penalty\ for all\penalty\ }w=1,\dots,m\right\}

(60)

are sets of eligible integer index tuples (i.e., tuples which appear in the series) and
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is defined in Equation (
57
) of Theorem
1
. The series (
59
) is absolutely convergent for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
if all Fourier coefficients
\left\{\mathbf{J}_{k}\right\}_{k=-2N,\dots,2N}
that occur in
\mathbf{H}
are bounded.

The proof of Theorem
2
is carried out in Section
5.3
.
The series expression for the direct Koopman-Hill projection of Equation (
49
) follows immediately:

Corollary 3

(Direct Koopman-Hill projection)
.

The direct Koopman-Hill projection approximation as defined in Equation (
49
),
\mathbf{C}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{Q}_{0}(t)
, is given by the series

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}

(61)

with the index parallelotopes

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}=\left\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:\left|\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}\right|\leq N\text{\penalty\ for all\penalty\ }w=1,\dots,m\right\}\;.

(62)

Proof.

This is a direct consequence of Theorem
2
for
j=0
.
∎

Geometrically, the eligible sets
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
form parallelotopes (generalized parallelograms) in
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, centered at the point
(j,0,\dots,0)
. In essence, the true fundamental solution sums over all possible integer index tuples, while the Koopman-Hill approximation only includes those tuples that lie within the corresponding
m
-dimensional parallelotope. Figure
3
illustrates these parallelotopes for the case
m=2
.

(a)

\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{-3}

(b)

\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{0}

(c)

\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{3}

Figure 3
:
Visualization of the sets
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(2)}
with
N=3
for different values of
j
. Center element located at
(j,0)
is indicated by a red dot.

Both series expressions of Equations (
56
) and (
61
) for the true fundamental solution matrix and its Koopman-Hill approximation are characterized by the same monomials
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
and identical scalar factors
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
. The only difference is that the Koopman-Hill approximation of Equation (
61
) is a partial sum of the true fundamental solution matrix of Equation (
56
) due to the truncation effects, only summing over entries of the eligible parallelotopes
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
instead of over the whole
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. Consequently, the truncation error between both can be expressed as the series over all multi-indices that are
not
included in the eligible parallelotopes, i.e.,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}(t)=\mathbf{\Phi}(t)-\mathbf{C}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;.

(63)

Our main result bounds Equation (
63
) to provide a convergence guarantee for the Koopman-Hill approximation.

Theorem 4

(Error bound and convergence of the direct Koopman-Hill projection approximation)
.

Let Assumption
1
hold with
b>\ln 2
. For a fixed truncation order
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the approximation error between the true fundamental solution matrix and the direct Koopman-Hill projection defined in Equation (
49
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\Phi}(t)-\mathbf{C}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}\right\|\leq(2\mathrm{e}^{-b})^{N}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\left|4at\right|}-1\right)\;.

(64)

This error bound decays exponentially with
N
, providing a convergence guarantee of the Koopman-Hill projection. Satisfaction of a desired accuracy
\left\|\mathbf{\Phi}(t)-\mathbf{C}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}\right\|\leq E_{\mathrm{des}}
is guaranteed if the truncation order fulfills

\displaystyle N\geq N^{*}=\frac{\left|4at\right|+\ln\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\left|4at\right|}\right)-\ln(E_{\mathrm{des}})}{b-\ln 2}\;.

(65)

Proof.

With Assumption
1
, a product
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
of Fourier coefficients is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|\leq\left\|\mathbf{J}_{p_{1}}\right\|\dots\left\|\mathbf{J}_{p_{m}}\right\|\leq a^{m}\mathrm{e}^{-b\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}\;.

(66)

This bound is coarse when the Fourier coefficient matrices are not diagonally dominant, but it reduces the involved matrix norms on
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
to a
1
-norm in the integer index tuple
\mathbf{p}
. Using this bound and Equation (
58
) on each summand of the approximation error given by Equation (
63
) individually yields

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{E}(t)\right\|\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}}\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}}\frac{\left|at\right|^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{e}^{-b\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}\;.

(67)

Equation (
67
) already contains two majorly conservative simplifications. Firstly, bounding every summand individually cancels any interaction between terms which could potentially annihilate each other. Secondly, every scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is bounded by its polynomial upper bound of Equation (
58
). From Figure
2
it is obvious that this bound is not particularly sharp as
t
increases. In fact, for the majority of values of
\mathbf{p}
,
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
is even periodic and bounded by a constant. This is examined in more detail in
B
. However, using any more sophisticated bounds for
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
or
\left\|\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|
complicates Equation (
67
) so much that it is unclear how to proceed to a simple closed-form expression.

The benefit of Equation (
67
) is that only the norm
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|
of each integer index tuple appears in the summand. The sum over all integer index tuples can thus be replaced by a sum over their norm. This reformulation yields the triple sum

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{E}(t)\right\|\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|at\right|^{m}}{m!}\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-bM}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{0}\\
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|=M\end{subarray}}1\;,

(68)

where the rightmost sum counts the number of integer
m
-tuples of a certain norm
M
that lie outside the parallelotope
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{0}
. This sum has only finitely many summands: The total number of integer index tuples of norm
M
is bounded by Lemma
13
ii)
of
C
, and we only sum over a subset of them.

(a)

N=2
: rhombus
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|\!=\!2\subset\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
.

(b)

N=4
: rhombus
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|=4\subset\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
.

Figure 4
:
Visualization of integer index set
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
(large dots). Inner rhombus (red), outer square (blue) and outer rhombus (black) are indicated.

In Figure
4
, the integer index tuples are visualized for
m=2
and and three different values of
N
. Visually, the space
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
in the figure can be separated into three regions, depending on the norm
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|
. Tuples within the
N
-rhombus shown in red in Figure
4
, i. e.,
\mathbf{p}
with small norm
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|\leq N
, are always elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
. In contrast, tuples
\mathbf{p}
are never elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
when
\max\{\left|p_{1}\right|,\left|p_{2}\right|\}>2N
, i.e., when
\mathbf{p}
lies outside the
2N
-square shown in blue in Figure
4
. In the intermediate region outside the
N
-rhombus but within the
2N
-square, some tuples are elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
and the others are not. It can be easily verified from the definition of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
in Equation (
60
) that these three observations generalize to arbitrary
m
: All tuples
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
inside the
N
-rhombus with norm
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|\leq N
are elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
, while no tuple outside the
2N
-hypercube with an entry
\left|p_{k}\right|>2N
can be an element of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
. In the intermediate region
N<\left|\mathbf{p}\right|
and
\left|p_{k}\right|\leq 2N
for all
k
, some tuples are elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
and the others are not.

This observation simplifies the rightmost summation in Equation (
68
) depending on
M
. For
M\leq N
, in the inner region, all possible tuples
\mathbf{p}
are elements of the eligible set
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
and the set over which to sum in Equation (
68
) is empty. Hence, the summation over
M
in Equation (
68
) can equivalently be initialized at
M=N+1
instead of
M=0
. For
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|>2mN
, shown in black in Figure
4
, at least one entry must have
\left|p_{k}\right|>2N
and no such tuples can be elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
. Hence, for
M>2mN
, the rightmost sum of Equation (
68
) must count all integer index tuples with norm
M
. Using Lemma
13
ii)
from
C
, this number is bounded by
2^{m}\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}
. In the intermediate region
N<M\leq 2mN
, counting is not so easy. As another very conservative simplification step, we upper-bound the rightmost sum in Equation (
68
) by the binomial coefficient
2^{m}\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}
also in this region. Essentially, we count integer index tuples in the intermediate region
N<M\leq 2mN
, irrespective of whether they are elements of
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
or not. These arguments simplify Equation (
68
) to

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{E}(t)\right\|\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|at\right|^{m}}{m!}\sum_{M=N+1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-bM}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\\
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|=M\end{subarray}}1\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}\sum_{M=N+1}^{\infty}\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}\mathrm{e}^{-bM}\;.

(69)

The coarseness introduced by this step increases as
N
and
m
increase and the intermediate region becomes larger. Being able to resolve the number of considered tuples more accurately in the intermediate region would likely improve the convergence rate of the error bound, possibly removing the factor
2^{m}
which is, in part, responsible for the condition
b>\ln 2
in Theorem
4
. However, this counting problem is non-trivial and beyond the scope of this paper.

The rightmost series in Equation (
69
) is the remainder
R_{N}(x)
of the Taylor series of
(1-x)^{-(k+1)}
, treated in Lemma
16
of
D
, with
x=\mathrm{e}^{-b}<1
and
k=m-1
. Using the expression (
178
) for the remainder and keeping
x
for the sake of brevity, we obtain

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{E}(t)\right\|\leq x^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}\sum_{w=1}^{m}\binom{N+m}{N+w}\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{w}\;.

(70)

Due to the infinite series over
m
, Equation (
70
) can still not be evaluated with ease and further bounding steps are necessary. The assumption
b>\ln 2
guarantees that
x<0.5
, and thus
\frac{x}{1-x}<1
can be neglected. This simplification does not introduce very much additional slack as
b
will often be chosen close to
\ln 2
in practice, see Section
6
. However, this still does not solve the issue of the infinite series. Summing the binomial coefficients with lower argument
0
to
N+m
instead of from
N+1
to
N+m
yields the bound

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{E}(t)\right\|

\displaystyle\leq x^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}\sum_{w=0}^{m+N}\binom{m+N}{w}

\displaystyle=x^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}2^{m+N}

\displaystyle=(2x)^{N}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{\left|4at\right|^{m}}{m!}

\displaystyle=(2\mathrm{e}^{-b})^{N}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\left|4at\right|}-1\right)\;.

(71)

This is the desired error bound of Equation (
64
). The expression (
65
) for the minimum truncation order
N^{*}
which guarantees an error smaller than
E_{\mathrm{des}}
follows immediately from Equation (
71
) by isolating
N^{*}
in
E_{\mathrm{des}}=(2\mathrm{e}^{-b})^{N^{*}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\left|4at\right|}-1\right)
.
∎

The bounding steps employed in the above proof to go from the series expression of Equation (
63
) to the closed-form bound of Equation (
71
) are conservative in multiple individual instances. On the one hand, every summand of Equation (
63
) is bounded individually and coarsely using the exponential decay condition. This leads to the
\mathrm{e}^{at}
term in Equation (
71
). Numerical experiments in Section
6
indicate that this term is not very sharp and might be an artifact of the specific proof strategy employed here, as the actual error does not seem to grow with
t
after a short initial phase. A more sophisticated bound could potentially leverage the periodicity of
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
for most values of
\mathbf{p}
, see also
B
.

On the other hand, counting the relevant integer tuples in the rightmost series in Equation (
70
) accurately is a challenging combinatorial problem. The very conservative upper bound chosen here leads to the binomial coefficient expression of Equation (
69
). This expression is beneficial because it can be identified with a certain Taylor series, but the subsequent handling of binomial coefficients leads to the technical requirement
b>\ln 2
. It is unclear how the remaining summations could be eliminated if a sharper bound for the number of considered integer indices were employed.

The condition
b>\ln 2
for the applicability of the closed-form bound of Equation (
64
) requires the Fourier coefficients of
\mathbf{J}(t)
to decay exponentially at a rate of at least
\ln 2
. This is a stricter requirement than the arbitrary exponential decay needed for absolute convergence of
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
, according to Theorem
1
. In practice, whether the condition
b>\ln 2
is satisfied can be checked numerically, as demonstrated in Section
6
with three numerical examples. Despite being not particularly sharp, the bound is the first of its kind and thus integral in providing theoretical backing for the Koopman-Hill projection method, which the Koopman framework cannot provide.

4
Improved convergence rate using subharmonic projection

According to Theorem
2
, the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(t)
contains
2N+1
blocks, each offering an individual approximation of the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
. Theorem
4
gives an explicit error bound for the centermost block
\mathbf{Q}_{0}
. The other blocks differ from
\mathbf{Q}_{0}
only by a shift in the center of their respective index parallelotopes
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
(see Figure
3
). In principle, the proof of Theorem
4
can be adapted to these other blocks, but counting the eligible indices in Equation (
68
) becomes even more complex when the parallelotope is not centered at the origin. Specifically, the maximum norm
M
for which all integer indices are inside
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
decreases as
\left|j\right|
increases, so the summation in Equation (
69
) must start at
N+1-\left|j\right|
instead of
N+1
. As a result, the error bound for each block decays with
N-\left|j\right|
rather than
N
, making the centermost block
\mathbf{Q}_{0}
the one with the smallest error bound.

However, previous works
[
1
,
2
]
have observed that better accuracy can be achieved in some cases by approximating the fundamental solution matrix by a linear combination over all blocks of
\mathbf{Q}(t)
.
In the present section, we formalize this approach, which we call the
subharmonic
approach, making it applicable to arbitrary dynamics fulfilling Assumption
1
. We show that this approach is not only beneficial numerically, but also comes with a lower error bound compared to the direct approach.

4.1
Subharmonic formulation and series expression

Consider a fixed truncation order
N\in\mathbb{N}
and an arbitrary
T
-periodic system matrix
\mathbf{J}(t)
fulfilling Assumption
1
with
b>\ln 2
. We make no further assumption on the original Fourier series given in Equation (
2
) with
\omega=\frac{2\pi}{T}
.
In particular, the Fourier coefficient matrices
\mathbf{J}_{k}
may be nonzero for even and odd values of
k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
As the system matrix
\mathbf{J}(t)
is
T
-periodic, it can also be considered
\tilde{T}=2T
-periodic. The corresponding Fourier series with the halved base frequency
\tilde{\omega}=\frac{2\pi}{2T}=\frac{\omega}{2}
is

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}(t)=\sum_{\tilde{k}=-\infty}^{\infty}\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{k}\frac{\omega}{2}t}\;.

(72)

Here and below, we denote quantities associated with the doubled period
\tilde{T}
and the Fourier series in Equation (
72
) with a tilde. Quantities without the tilde refer to the original Fourier series in Equation (
2
).
Comparison of the Fourier series expressions in Equations (
2
) and (
72
) reveals that every odd subharmonic Fourier coefficient vanishes:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{J}_{\frac{\tilde{k}}{2}}&\text{if\penalty\ }\tilde{k}\text{\penalty\ even}\\
\mathbf{0}&\text{if\penalty\ }\tilde{k}\text{\penalty\ odd}\;.\end{cases}

(73)

The Fourier coefficients of Equation (
73
) in the Fourier series of Equation (
72
) fulfill Assumption
1
with
\tilde{a}=a
and
\tilde{b}=\frac{b}{2}
. The subharmonic Hill matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
of truncation order
\tilde{N}
can be constructed from the subharmonic Fourier series of Equation (
72
) in the usual way. According to Equation (
28
), the subharmonic Fourier coefficients
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}
with
{\tilde{k}\in\left\{-2\tilde{N},\dots,2\tilde{N}\right\}}
are placed on the corresponding block diagonals and purely imaginary terms of the form
\mathrm{i}\tilde{k}\tilde{\omega}
are added in the main diagonal.

The (classical) Hill matrix
\mathbf{H}
of truncation order
N
associated with the original Fourier series in Equation (
2
) and the subharmonic Hill matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
of truncation order
\tilde{N}=2N
associated with the subharmonic Fourier series in Equation (
72
) both contain the same set of Fourier coefficient matrices
\left\{\mathbf{J}_{-2N},\dots,\mathbf{J}_{2N}\right\}
and are thus, in a sense, related. The difference between
\mathbf{H}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
, however, is that the subharmonic Hill matrix is of twice the size of the original Hill matrix and the blocks of nontrivial Fourier coefficient matrices are separated by blocks of zeros (
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}
for odd
\tilde{k}
). Explicitly for
N=1
, this yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{lll}\hbox to20.4pt{\vbox to20.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.20001pt\lower-12.35278pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}

Note that both Equations (
4.1
) and (
4.1
) are expressed in terms of the
original
Fourier coefficients pertaining to the Fourier series of Equation (
2
). In the even row blocks of
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
(counted starting from the centermost row block with index
0
), only the even column blocks are nonzero. If all odd row and column blocks are discarded, exactly the original Hill matrix
\mathbf{H}
with
2N+1\times 2N+1
blocks remains. Similarly, only the odd column blocks are nonzero in the odd row blocks of
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
. If all even row and column blocks are discarded, what remains is a matrix
\hat{\mathbf{H}}
with
2N
blocks. Explicitly for
N=1
, the matrix
\hat{\mathbf{H}}
is

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{H}}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{llll}\hbox to20.4pt{\vbox to20.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 10.20001pt\lower-12.35278pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B9B9B9} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#B9B9B9} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -13.84 -16.82 h 27.67 v 27.67 h -27.67 Z}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.21527pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -7.22 -2.98)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
