{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-5.21527pt}{-2.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -7.22 -2.98)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}-\hbox to25.74pt{\vbox to20.4pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 12.86783pt\lower-10.20001pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#FFFFFF} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray={3.0pt,3.0pt},stroke-dashoffset=0.0pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -17.53 -13.84 h 35.06 v 27.67 h -35.06 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-10.66783pt}{-3.43056pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -14.76 -4.75)} \pgfsys@hbox{58}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{array}\right)\;.

For all values of
N
, the matrix
\hat{\mathbf{H}}
can be constructed from
\mathbf{H}
by discarding the last block row and block column and subtracting
0.5\mathrm{i}\omega
from the diagonal.

By also defining the diagonal matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}={\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}}\left\{-N,-N+\frac{1}{2},N-1,\dots,N-\frac{1}{2},N\right\}\otimes\mathbf{I}
of the corresponding subharmonic size, we establish the subharmonic equivalent to the matrix
\mathbf{Q}(t)
of Equation (
48
),

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega t\tilde{\mathbf{D}}}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\mathbf{H}}t}\tilde{\mathbf{W}}\;,

(84)

which has
2\tilde{N}+1=4N+1
blocks of size
n\times n
that approximate the fundamental solution matrix due to Theorem
2
. Naively, with Theorem
4
, the approximation error of the centermost block of
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}
is

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{0}-\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\right|\leq(2\mathrm{e}^{-\tilde{b}})^{\tilde{N}}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\left|4at\right|}-1\right)=(2\mathrm{e}^{-b})^{N}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\left|4at\right|}-1\right)\;,

(85)

so no improvements in accuracy are expected compared to the original, non-subharmonic formulation if only the centermost block is considered. The following lemma provides a series expression for all blocks of
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)
specifically when
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
is of subharmonic structure, showing in particular that
\mathbf{Q}_{0}(t)
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{0}(t)
are indeed identical.

Lemma 5

(Series expression for
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)
in subharmonic formulation)
.

Let
\mathbf{J}(t)
be a
T
-periodic system matrix, whose original (non-subharmonic) Fourier series as defined in Equation (
2
) has the Fourier coefficients
\mathbf{J}_{-2N},\dots,\mathbf{J}_{2N}
which may all be nonzero. Consider the matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)
constructed via Equation (
84
) using the corresponding subharmonic Hill matrix of the form of Equation (
4.1
). For any
\tilde{j}\in\left\{-2N,\dots,2N\right\}
, the
\tilde{j}
-th
n\times n
-block of
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)
is given by the series

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;,

(86)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}}^{(m)}

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:\left|\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}-\sum_{i=1}^{w}p_{i}\right|\leq N\text{\penalty\ for all\penalty\ }w=1,\dots m\right\}

(87)

are the sets of eligible integer index tuples,
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
refers to products of the original (non-subharmonic) Fourier coefficient matrices occurring in Equation (
2
), and
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is defined in Equation (
57
) of Theorem
1
. The series in Equation (
86
) is absolutely convergent for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
if all Fourier coefficients
\left\{\mathbf{J}_{k}\right\}_{k=-2N,\dots,2N}
of the original (non-subharmonic) Fourier series are bounded.

The proof of Theorem
5
is deferred to Section
5.4
, as with the other series proofs.

The index set defined in Equation (
87
) generalizes the eligible index set
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
from Theorem
2
(cf. Equation (
60
)) to non-integer values of
j
. Any block with even
\tilde{j}
, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}}
with
\tilde{j}=2j
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, is identical to the corresponding block
\mathbf{Q}_{j}
of the non-subharmonic
\mathbf{Q}(t)
from Theorem
2
. In particular, the central blocks
\mathbf{Q}_{0}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{0}
coincide.

For odd
\tilde{j}
, the corresponding set
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}}^{(m)}
is the intersection of the index sets
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}+1}{2}}^{(m)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}-1}{2}}^{(m)}
, evaluated at the integer values adjacent to
\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}
. This follows from the fact that equality in the “
\leq
” condition of Equation (
87
) can never be attained when
\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}
is not integer, as visualized in Figure
5(a)
for the case
m=2
. The set difference between
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}}^{(m)}
and
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}+1}{2}}^{(m)}
then forms an “outer edge” of the larger set
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}+1}{2}}^{(m)}
.
The difference
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}+1}-\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}}
between two neighboring blocks of
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}
sums exactly over this set difference for all
m
. In other words, it isolates all integer index tuples in the series that lie on a specific edge of a parallelotope.

(a)

Sets
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{2}
(black) and
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{1.5}
(red) for
N=2
and their boundaries. Set difference is an edge of
\mathcal{P}^{(2)}_{2}
.

(b)

Set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(2)}
(large dots) for
N=4
, constructed by
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
(blue) and edges of
\mathcal{P}_{\pm j}^{(2)}
,
j=1,\dots,4
(yellow, green, red, purple).

Figure 5
:
Construction of the subharmonic index set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(2)}
as the union of sets
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(2)}
,
j=-N,\dots,N
.

As a result, an approximation of the fundamental solution matrix with significantly enlarged index sets can be constructed by taking an alternating superposition of the blocks in
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{subh}}(t):=\tilde{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{subh}}\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{subh}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tilde{\mathbf{D}}t}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\mathbf{H}}t}\tilde{\mathbf{W}}

(88a)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{subh}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I},-\mathbf{I},\mathbf{I},\dots,-\mathbf{I},\mathbf{I}\right)

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n(4N+1)}

(88b)

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{W}}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{I},\dots\mathbf{I}\right)^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}

\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R}^{n(4N+1)\times n}\;.

(88c)

Equation (
88
) is termed the subharmonic Koopman-Hill projection formula, which has been shown numerically to provide improved accuracy
[
2
]
. The series expression for this formulation is formalized in the following theorem.

Theorem 6

(Series expression for
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{subh}}
)
.

The series expression for the subharmonic Koopman-Hill projection formulation, with
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}}
denoting the blocks of
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}
in Equation (
84
), is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{subh}}(t):=\sum_{\tilde{j}=-2N}^{2N}(-1)^{\tilde{j}}\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}

(89)

with the integer index sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(m)}=\bigcup_{j=-N}^{N}\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}=\left\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:\left|\sum_{l=v}^{w}p_{l}\right|\leq 2N\text{\penalty\ for all\penalty\ }v,w=1,\dots,m\right\}\;.

(90)

The series converges absolutely for all
t\in\mathbb{R}
.
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
refers to products of the original (non-subharmonic) Fourier coefficient matrices occurring in Equation (
2
).

Proof.

By reformulating Equation (
89
), the series
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{subh}}(t)
is given by

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{subh}}(t)=\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{0}(t)+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2j}(t)-\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{2j-1}(t)\right)+\left(\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{-2j}(t)-\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{-2j+1}(t)\right)\;.

(91)

As discussed above, the integer index set
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{\pm(j-\frac{1}{2})}
corresponding to the block
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pm(2j-1)}
is a strict subset of
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{\pm j}
, only excluding the “outer” edge. Therefore, the difference between blocks
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pm 2j}
and
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pm(2j-1)}
provides the summation over only the outer edge of the sets
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{\pm j}
,
m=1,\dots,\infty
. This idea is visualized in Figure
5(b)
, with the set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(2)}
constituted of the set
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
due to the block
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{0}
, and then additional edges to the left and right due to the summands
(\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pm 2j}-\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\pm(2j-1)})
for
j=1,\dots,N
. All these subsets are disjoint, and the whole summation in Equation (
91
) is given by summing over the union of the edges, or equivalently over the union of the
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
for
j=-N,\dots,N
.

It remains to show that this union of the sets
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
is described by the set expression in Equation (
90
). First, consider
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{j}
for an arbitrary
j\in\left\{-N,\dots,N\right\}
. With

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{l=v}^{w}p_{l}\right|=\left|(j-\sum_{l=1}^{v-1}p_{l})-(j-\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l})\right|\leq N+N\;,

(92)

we see immediately that all sets
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
,
j=-N,\dots,N
, are subsets of the expression in Equation (
90
). For the inverse direction, let
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
satisfy
\left|\sum_{l=v}^{w}p_{l}\right|\leq 2N
for all natural
v<w\leq m
.

Consider first the case where

\displaystyle\min_{w}\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}\geq 0\;.

(93)

Then, the chain of inequalities

\displaystyle-N\leq-N+\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}\leq-N+2N=N

(94)

holds for all
w=1,\dots,m
, asserting that
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{N}
. Otherwise, if the minimal sum is negative, choose

\displaystyle j=N+\min_{w}\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}

(95)

and denote the minimizer of Equation (
95
) by
w^{*}
. Clearly,
-N\leq j<N
.
The chain of inequalities

\displaystyle N=j-\sum_{l=1}^{w^{*}}p_{l}\geq j-\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}=N-\left(\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}-\sum_{l=1}^{w^{*}}p_{l}\right)\geq-N

(96)

holds for all
w=1,\dots,m
, asserting that
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{j}
.
∎

4.2
Implementation of the subharmonic formulation

Before calculating the error bound of the series in Equation (
89
), we make a few remarks about the efficient computation of
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{subh}}(t)
.
The matrix
\tilde{\mathbf{H}}
is twice as large as the equivalent non-subharmonic matrix
\mathbf{H}
(cf. Equations (
4.1
) and (
4.1
)). Hence, if Equation (
84
) were implemented directly as written, the matrix exponential should approximately require the
2^{3}=8
-fold computation time compared to the one in Equation (
49
). However, due to the many zeros introduced by construction of the subharmonic Hill matrix, it can be decoupled into two sub-matrices of sizes
n(2N+1)
and
2nN
by the orthogonal similarity transform
\mathbf{M}\tilde{\mathbf{H}}\mathbf{M}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}
, where
\mathbf{M}
is a permutation matrix that collects first all uneven row blocks of the identity matrix, and afterwards all even ones.
This similarity transform results in a block diagonal matrix. Exemplarily with
N=1
, the transformed subharmonic Hill matrix is (all empty entries are zero):

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{H}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{M}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{J}_{0}+\mathrm{i}\omega&\mathbf{J}_{-1}&\mathbf{J}_{-2}\\
\mathbf{J}_{1}&\mathbf{J}_{0}&\mathbf{J}_{-1}\\
\mathbf{J}_{2}&\mathbf{J}_{1}&\mathbf{J}_{0}-\mathrm{i}\omega&\\
&&&\mathbf{J}_{0}+0.5\mathrm{i}\omega&\mathbf{J}_{-1}\\
&&&\mathbf{J}_{1}&\mathbf{J}_{0}-0.5\mathrm{i}\omega\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}\;.

(97)

By close inspection, the first block of size
n(2N+1)\times n(2N+1)
is exactly
\mathbf{H}
, i.e., the “original” Hill matrix associated with the Fourier series in Equation (
2
). The second block
\hat{\mathbf{H}}
of size
2nN\times 2nN
can easily be constructed from the original matrix
\mathbf{H}
by discarding the last row and column block and then subtracting
0.5\mathrm{i}\omega
from the diagonal elements. Similarly, reordering the elements of
\tilde{\mathbf{D}}
using
\mathbf{M}
separates the larger diagonal matrix into the original
\mathbf{D}
in the first half and a diagonal matrix
\hat{\mathbf{D}}
in the second half, which is constructed from
\mathbf{D}
by removing the last
n
rows and columns and subtracting
0.5\mathrm{i}\omega
on the diagonal. The Koopman-Hill approximation then becomes

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)=\mathbf{M}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}\begin{pmatrix}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}&\mathbf{0}\\
\mathbf{0}&\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\hat{\mathbf{D}}t}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{\mathbf{H}}t}\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{M}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}\tilde{\mathbf{W}}\;.

(98)

The matrix exponentials of the two blocks, which are each (at most) the size of the original Hill matrix, can be evaluated independently.
Reordering the block rows of
\tilde{\mathbf{W}}
using
\mathbf{M}
does not have any effect as all block rows are the identity matrix. Summing the blocks of
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}
with alternating sign is equivalent to adding all entries of the first exponential block, while subtracting the others pertaining to the second block. Thus, we obtain the formula for the monodromy matrix with subharmonic Koopman-Hill projection

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\approx\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{subh}}(t)=\mathbf{W}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}+\hat{\mathbf{W}}^{\mathop{\mathrm{T}}}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\hat{\mathbf{D}}t}\mathrm{e}^{\hat{\mathbf{H}}t}\hat{\mathbf{W}}\;,

(99)

where
\mathbf{W}
is a stack of
2N+1
identity matrices as before, and
\hat{\mathbf{W}}
is a stack of
2N
identity matrices.
As two matrix exponentials of almost same size must be evaluated, this procedure is about twice as costly as evaluating the naive projection.

4.3
Convergence of the subharmonic formulation

In this section, we analyze the convergence of the subharmonic Koopman-Hill projection of Equation (
99
) using its series expression (
89
) and the tools of the previous sections. As in Section
3
, we want to bound

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{E}}(t)=\mathbf{\Phi}(t)-\left(\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\setminus\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;.

(100)

(a)

N=2
.

(b)

N=4
.

Figure 6
:
Visualization of integer index set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(2)}
(large dots) for the subharmonic projection.
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(2)}
is indicated by gray dots and additional indices only present in the subharmonic formulation by black dots. Inner rhombus (red), outer square (blue) and outer rhombus (black) are indicated.

Analogously to Figure
4
, Figure
6
shows the sets
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(2)}
for different truncation orders
N
as large dots. Hereby, tuples
\mathbf{p}
that are also contained in
cP_{0}
, and thus in the direct projection of Corollary
3
, are shown in gray while tuples that are additionally included due to the subharmonic formulation are shown in black. In the figures, the largest rhombus that is contained within
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(2)}
, shown in red, is of size
2N
, twice as large as in the direct projection (cf. Figure
4
). This also follows immediately from the definition (
90
) for arbitrary
m
. The size of the outer rhombus remains the same, compared to the direct, non-subharmonic case.
We can thus repeat the bounding steps of Section
3
, but with the size
N
of the inner rhombus replaced by
2N
during the counting of nonzero summands. This yields an analogous error bound, formulated in the following theorem.

Theorem 7

(Convergence and error bound of the subharmonic Koopman-Hill projection)
.

Let Assumption
1
hold with
b>\ln 2
. For a fixed truncation order
N\in\mathbb{N}
, the approximation error between the true fundamental solution matrix and the subharmonic Koopman-Hill projection defined in Equation (
88
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbf{\Phi}(t)-\tilde{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{subh}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tilde{\mathbf{D}}t}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\mathbf{H}}t}\tilde{\mathbf{W}}\right\|\leq(2\mathrm{e}^{-b})^{2N}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\left|4at\right|}-1\right)\;.

(101)

This error bound decays exponentially with
N
, providing a convergence guarantee of the subharmonic Koopman-Hill projection. Satisfaction of a desired accuracy
\left\|\mathbf{\Phi}(t)-\tilde{\mathbf{C}}_{\mathrm{subh}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\tilde{\mathbf{D}}t}\mathrm{e}^{\tilde{\mathbf{H}}t}\tilde{\mathbf{W}}\right\|\leq E_{\mathrm{des}}
is guaranteed if the truncation order fulfills

\displaystyle N\geq\tilde{N}^{*}=\frac{\left|4at\right|+\ln\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-\left|4at\right|}\right)-\ln(E_{\mathrm{des}})}{2(b-\ln 2)}\;.

(102)

Proof.

The proof follows by replacing
N
by
2N
in Equations (
69
) – (
71
) within the proof of Theorem
4
.
∎

Remark
.

The bound in Equation (
101
) decays exponentially with twice the decay rate as the corresponding bound (
64
) of the direct formulation.

5
Derivative-based proofs for the series expressions

This section is dedicated to proving Theorems
1
and
2
, which were stated but left unproven in Section
3
. Both proofs follow the same idea: Showing that the series expressions of Equations (
56
) and (
59
) solve associated linear matrix initial value problems whose solutions are given by
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
and
\mathbf{Q}(t)
, respectively. Once absolute convergence is established, the series expressions can be differentiated summand by summand. In each summand of Equation (
56
) or (
59
), the only time-dependent term is the scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
. Hence, an expression for
\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
is required in the left-hand side of each matrix initial value problem. Section
5.1
provides a recursive relationship between
\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
and
\xi_{[p2,\dots,p_{m}]}(t)
. Leveraging this relationship, Sections
5.2
and
5.3
provide proofs for Theorems
1
and
2
, respectively. Finally, Section
5.4
provides the proof of Theorem
5
, using Theorem
2
.

5.1
Bound and derivatives of the scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

As a preliminary step for the analysis of the series expressions in Equations (
56
) and (
59
) of Theorems
1
and
2
, useful properties of the scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
are established in this section. First, the following lemma isolates and proves the polynomial bound on the absolute value of the scalar factor that is depicted in Figure
2
and mentioned in Theorem
1
. Afterwards, a recursive differential relationship for the
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
with varying lengths of
\mathbf{p}
is established.

Lemma 8

(Bound of
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
)
.

For all
m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
,
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, and
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the series in Equation (
57
) over all multi-indices of length
m
is absolutely convergent and bounded by Equation (
58
).

Proof.

First we establish absolute convergence. Using for all
k=1,\dots,m
the bound

\displaystyle\left(p_{1}+\dots+p_{k}\right)^{\alpha_{k}}\leq\left(\left|p_{1}\right|+\dots+\left|p_{k}\right|\right)^{\alpha_{k}}\leq\left(\left|p_{1}\right|+\dots+\left|p_{k}\right|+\dots+\left|p_{m}\right|\right)^{\alpha_{k}}=\left|\mathbf{p}\right|^{\alpha_{k}}\;,

(103)

the multi-index expression in Equation (
57
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\left[p_{1},p_{1}+p_{2},\dots,p_{1}+\dots+p_{m}\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\leq\left|\mathbf{p}\right|^{(\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{m})}=\left|\mathbf{p}\right|^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}\;.

(104)

We denote summands of Equation (
57
) by

\displaystyle s_{\bm{\alpha}}:=\frac{t^{m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{(m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}\left[p_{1},p_{1}+p_{2},\dots,p_{1}+\dots+p_{m}\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\;.

(105)

With Equation (
104
), the summands are bounded via

\displaystyle\left|s_{\bm{\alpha}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left|t\right|^{m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{(m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!}\omega^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}\left|\mathbf{p}\right|^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}\;.

(106)

As the right-hand side only depends on the
1
-norm
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|
and not on the actual value of
\bm{\alpha}
itself, the sum over all
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
can be expressed by a sum over all norms
M:=\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|\in\mathbb{N}
, where we count for each
M
the number of tuples
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
with that norm. Using Lemma
13
i)
from
C
, there are exactly
\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}
such tuples for a given norm
M
. This simplifies Equation (
106
) to

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\left|s_{\bm{\alpha}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\\
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|=M\end{subarray}}\frac{\left|t\right|^{m+M}}{(m+M)!}\omega^{M}\left|\mathbf{p}\right|^{M}

(107)

\displaystyle=\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\frac{(M+m-1)!}{M!(m-1)!}\frac{\left|t\right|^{m+M}}{(m+M)(m+M-1)!}\omega^{M}\left|\mathbf{p}\right|^{M}

(108)

\displaystyle=\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|t\right|^{m}}{(m-1)!(m+M)}\frac{(\left|\mathbf{p}\right|\left|\omega t\right|)^{M}}{M!}\;.

(109)

After using
(m+M)\geq m
to pull out the first factor, and identifying the exponential series in the second factor, the bound

\displaystyle\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\left|s_{\bm{\alpha}}\right|\leq\frac{\left|t\right|^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{e}^{\left|\mathbf{p}\right|\left|\omega t\right|}<\infty

(110)

follows. The bound of Equation (
110
) is very coarse since bounding each summand
s_{\bm{\alpha}}
individually ignores potential cancellations due to
(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{\bm{\alpha}}
for different values of
\bm{\alpha}
, but it suffices to establish absolute convergence. We can hence derive the sharper bound in Equation (
58
) by considering the actual values of
s_{\bm{\alpha}}
rather than just their magnitudes. Using

(m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!\geq(m!\,\alpha_{1}!\,\dots\alpha_{m}!)
, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\frac{t^{m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{m!\,\alpha_{1}!\,\dots\alpha_{m}!}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}\left[p_{1},p_{1}+p_{2},\dots,p_{1}+\dots+p_{m}\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{t^{m}}{m!}\left(\sum_{\alpha_{1}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{i}\omega p_{1}t)^{\alpha_{1}}}{\alpha_{1}!}\right)\left(\sum_{\alpha_{2}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{i}\omega(p_{1}+p_{2})t)^{\alpha_{2}}}{\alpha_{2}!}\right)\dots\left(\sum_{\alpha_{m}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{i}\omega(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})t)^{\alpha_{m}}}{\alpha_{m}!}\right)\right|\;.

(111)

where the sums were reorganized in the second step into factors that each only depend on one
\alpha_{k}
. In every round bracket of Equation (
111
), the power series of the exponential function can be identified. This simplifies the expression to

\displaystyle\left|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right|\leq\left|\frac{t^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega p_{1}t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(p_{1}+p_{2})t}\dots\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})t}\right|=\frac{\left|t\right|^{m}}{m!}\;.

(112)

∎

Due to Lemma
8
, the expression for
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
may be differentiated summand by summand. This leads after some reorganization to a recursive differential relationship between the scalar factors for various lengths of
\mathbf{p}
, covered in the following lemma.

Lemma 9

(Derivative of
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
)
.

The derivative of
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
fulfills the following relations:

1)

For
m=1
,
p\in\mathbb{Z}
it holds that
\dot{\xi}_{p}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\omega t}
.

2)

For
m>1,\mathbf{p}=[p_{1},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
it holds that
\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}(t)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{1}\omega t}
.

Proof.

For
m=1
, the derivative of Equation (
57
) becomes

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{p}(t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\sum_{\alpha\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{t^{\alpha+1}}{(\alpha+1)!}\left(\mathrm{i}\omega p\right)^{\alpha}\right)=\sum_{\alpha=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{\alpha}}{\alpha!}(\mathrm{i}\omega p)^{\alpha}=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\omega t}\;.

(113)

For
m\geq 2
, consider a fixed
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. We define partial sums of
\mathbf{p}
, excluding
p_{1}
, by
b_{k}:=\sum_{l=2}^{k}p_{l}
for all
k=2,\dots,m
. With the power series expression for the exponential

\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{1}\omega t}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}=0}^{\infty}\frac{(\mathrm{i}p_{1}\omega t)^{\alpha_{1}}}{\alpha_{1}!}
and the explicit expression for
\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}(t)
of Equation (
57
), the right-hand side of the differential relationship in Lemma
9
-
2
) becomes

\displaystyle\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}(t)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{1}\omega t}=\sum_{\alpha_{1}=0}^{\infty}\,\sum_{[\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{m}]\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\,\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}+\dots+\alpha_{m}}}{(m-1+\alpha_{2}+\dots+\alpha_{m})!\,\alpha_{1}!}\,p_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}b_{2}^{\alpha_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{\alpha_{m}}\;.

(114)

In contrast, differentiation of the power series in Equation (
57
) summand by summand yields the left-hand side

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{(m-1+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!}p_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}(p_{1}+b_{2})^{\alpha_{2}}\dots(p_{1}+b_{m})^{\alpha_{m}}\;.

(115)

The crucial difference between these two expressions is that the first integer index
p_{1}
occurs in every factor in Equation (
115
), while it only occurs isolated in Equation (
114
). In addition,
\alpha_{1}
is part of the large factorial in the denominator of Equation (
115
), but is isolated in Equation (
114
).
To prove equivalence of these two series expressions, we will proceed to pull the index
p_{1}
outside of every binomial in Equation (
115
) and reorder the summations.

Applying the binomial theorem to every factor in Equation (
115
) yields

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{(m-1+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!}p_{1}^{\alpha_{1}}\left(\sum_{n_{2}=0}^{\alpha_{2}}\binom{\alpha_{2}}{n_{2}}p_{1}^{\alpha_{2}-n_{2}}b_{2}^{n_{2}}\right)\dots\left(\sum_{n_{m}=0}^{\alpha_{m}}\binom{\alpha_{m}}{n_{m}}p_{1}^{\alpha_{m}-n_{m}}b_{m}^{n_{m}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}}\sum_{n_{2}=0}^{\alpha_{2}}\dots\sum_{n_{m}=0}^{\alpha_{m}}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{(m-1+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!}p_{1}^{\left(\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|-\sum_{k=2}^{m}n_{k}\right)}\,b_{2}^{n_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{n_{m}}\,\binom{\alpha_{2}}{n_{2}}\dots\binom{\alpha_{m}}{n_{m}}\;.

(116)

Using
\sum_{\alpha_{k}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{n_{k}=0}^{\alpha_{k}}=\sum_{n_{k}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{k}=n_{k}}^{\infty}
for
k=2,\dots,m
, every corresponding pair of sums can be swapped. We introduce the new summation multi-index
\mathbf{n}=[n_{2},\dots,n_{m}]
, which starts with
n_{2}
. Correspondingly, the shifted index
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}=\left[\tilde{\alpha}_{2},\dots,\tilde{\alpha}_{m}\right]\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}
with
\tilde{\alpha}_{k}=\alpha_{k}-n_{k}
also begins with
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
. Substituted into the series after the sums are swapped, this yields

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{\alpha_{2}=n_{2}}^{\infty}\dots\sum_{\alpha_{m}=n_{m}}^{\infty}\sum_{\alpha_{1}=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|}}{(m-1+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|)!}p_{1}^{\left(\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|-\sum_{k=2}^{m}n_{k}\right)}\,b_{2}^{n_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{n_{m}}\,\binom{\alpha_{2}}{n_{2}}\dots\binom{\alpha_{m}}{n_{m}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{\alpha_{1}=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{\alpha_{1}+\left|\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right|+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}}{(m-1+\alpha_{1}+\left|\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right|+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|)!}p_{1}^{\alpha_{1}+\left|\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right|}\,b_{2}^{n_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{n_{m}}\,\binom{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}+n_{2}}{n_{2}}\dots\binom{\tilde{\alpha}_{m}+n_{m}}{n_{m}}\;.

(117)

Next, the summation variable
\alpha_{1}
is replaced by
M=\alpha_{1}+\left|\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right|
to yield

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{M=\left|\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right|}^{\infty}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}}{(m-1+M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|)!}p_{1}^{M}\,b_{2}^{n_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{n_{m}}\,\binom{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}+n_{2}}{n_{2}}\dots\binom{\tilde{\alpha}_{m}+n_{m}}{n_{m}}\;.

(118)

Note that only the binomial coefficients and the lower bound of
M
depend on the multi-index
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}
. In the next step, we intend to swap the summation over
M
with the summation over
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}
. For the sake of brevity, we temporarily collect all
\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}
-independent terms of the summand into

\displaystyle h(t,M,\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p}):=\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}}{(m-1+M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|)!}p_{1}^{M}\,b_{2}^{n_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{n_{m}}\;.

(119)

For all summands it holds that
M\geq\left|\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right|
. Hence, the value of
M
acts as an upper bound on
\left|\tilde{\bm{\alpha}}\right|
. In other words, for any fixed value
M
,
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
can take any value that is not larger than
M
. Then, for fixed
M
and
\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
, the value of
\tilde{\alpha}_{3}
can be at most
M-\tilde{\alpha}_{2}
, and this procedure can be continued to pull the summation over
M
to the front and yield

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}h(t,M,\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\sum_{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}=0}^{M}\sum_{\tilde{\alpha}_{3}=0}^{M-\tilde{\alpha}_{2}}\dots\sum_{\tilde{\alpha}_{m}=0}^{M-\tilde{\alpha}_{2}-\dots-\tilde{\alpha}_{m-1}}\,\binom{\tilde{\alpha}_{2}+n_{2}}{n_{2}}\dots\binom{\tilde{\alpha}_{m}+n_{m}}{n_{m}}\;.

(120)

The
m-1
right-most summations are covered by Lemma
15
of
C
, so all summation indices
\tilde{\alpha}_{k}
for
k=2,\dots,m
can be eliminated:

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}h(t,M,\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})\binom{M+(m-1)+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}{M}\;.

(121)

Re-substituting
h(t,M,\mathbf{n},\mathbf{p})
and writing the binomial coefficient in factorial form yields

\displaystyle\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}}{(m-1+M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|)!}p_{1}^{M}\,b_{2}^{n_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{n_{m}}\frac{(M+(m-1)+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|)!}{M!(m-1+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|)!}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{N}^{m-1}}\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\frac{t^{m-1}(\mathrm{i}\omega t)^{M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}}{(m-1+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|)!M!}p_{1}^{M}\,b_{2}^{n_{2}}\dots b_{m}^{n_{m}}\;.

(122)

Comparison of Equation (
122
) to Equation (
114
) reveals
\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}(t)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{1}\omega t}
, concluding Case
2
.
∎

B
examines a consequence of Lemma
9
, namely that
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
is
T
-periodic with finite support of Fourier coefficients for most values of
\mathbf{p}
. This periodicity property is, however, not of immediate use for the further developments and is therefore deferred to the appendix.
In the next subsection, we use Lemma
9
directly as a tool to show that the series representation (
56
) fulfills the differential equation (
3
).

5.2
Proof of Theorem
1
: Series expression for
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)

In this section, we prove Theorem
1
by verifying that the series expression of Equation (
56
) satisfies the matrix initial value problem of Equation (
3
), which uniquely defines the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
. The proof relies heavily on the derivative relationship for
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
from Lemma
9
of the previous section.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

We begin by showing that the series in Equation (
56
) of Theorem
1
is absolutely convergent, i.e., we want show that

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}}\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|<\infty\;.

(123)

In a second step we will show that the series in Equation (
56
) solves the matrix initial value problem of Equation (
3
), which defines the fundamental solution matrix.

By Assumption
1
, the product
\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
of Fourier coefficients is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|=\left\|\mathbf{J}_{p_{1}}\dots\mathbf{J}_{p_{m}}\right\|\leq a^{m}\mathrm{e}^{b(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})}\;.

(124)

Together with the bound for
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
from Lemma
8
, each summand of Equation (
124
) is bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|\leq\left|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\right|\left\|\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|\leq\frac{\left|t\right|^{m}}{m!}a^{m}\mathrm{e}^{-b\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}=\frac{\left|at\right|^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{e}^{-b\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}\;.

(125)

Here, the matrix norm is reduced to a
1
-norm of the integer index tuple
\mathbf{p}
. As in the derivation of the error bound (Theorem
4
), the sum over all integer index tuples
\mathbf{p}
can be simplified to the sum over their norm
M=\left|\mathbf{p}\right|
, using Lemma
13
ii)
of
C
to count the number of tuples with a given norm
M
. This yields

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|\leq\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|at\right|^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{e}^{-bM}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}\\
\left|\mathbf{p}\right|=M\end{subarray}}1\leq\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}\mathrm{e}^{-bM}\;.

(126)

After pulling out the factor
\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}
, the rightmost series in Equation (
126
) is of the form
\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\binom{M+k}{k}M^{q}x^{M}
with
q=0
,
k=m-1
and
x=\mathrm{e}^{-b}<1
. This is the Taylor series of
(1-x)^{-(1+k)}
, treated in Lemma
16
of
D
. Hence, Equation (
126
) simplifies to

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|\leq\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}\mathrm{e}^{-bM}=\frac{\left|2at\right|^{m}}{m!}\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{-b}\right)^{-m}\;.

(127)

Absolute convergence follows by

\displaystyle 1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|\leq 1+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\left(\frac{\left|2at\right|}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-b}}\right)^{m}=\exp\left(\frac{\left|2at\right|}{1-\mathrm{e}^{-b}}\right)<\infty\;.

(128)

With the absolute convergence established, we are now ready to show that Equation (
56
) satisfies the matrix initial value problem of Equation (
3
).
As
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(0)=0
for arbitrary
m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
, the initial condition
\mathbf{\Phi}(0)=\mathbf{I}
is trivially satisfied. Differentiation of the series summand by summand yields the derivative

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{\Phi}}(t)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;,

(129)

and the right-hand side of the matrix initial value problem of Equation (
3
) reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}(t)\mathbf{\Phi}(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathbf{J}_{k}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\omega t}\right)\left(\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\omega t}\mathbf{J}_{p}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\omega t}\mathbf{J}_{k}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;.

(130)

With new integer index tuples
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}:=[k,\mathbf{p}]\in\mathbb{Z}^{m+1}
and the index shift
\tilde{m}=m+1
, this expression can be reformulated to

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}(t)\mathbf{\Phi}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=-\infty}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\omega t}\mathbf{J}_{p}+\sum_{\tilde{m}=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\in\mathbb{Z}^{\tilde{m}}}\xi_{[\tilde{p}_{2},\dots,\tilde{p}_{\tilde{m}]}}(t)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{p}_{1}\omega t}\mathcal{J}_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}}\;.

(131)

Comparison of Equations (
129
) and (
131
) reveals that the matrix initial value problem of Equation (
3
) is satisfied if the following conditions on
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
hold:

1)

\dot{\xi}_{p}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\omega t}
if
p\in\mathbb{Z}

2)

\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p_{1}\omega t}
if
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
with
m\geq 2
.

As these conditions are true due to Lemma
9
, the series of Equation (
56
) does indeed solve the matrix initial value problem for the fundamental solution matrix. Since the solution of a linear initial value problem is unique, the series of Equation (
56
) must thus be the fundamental solution matrix.
∎

5.3
Proof of Theorem
2
: Series expression for Koopman-Hill approximation
\mathbf{Q}(t)

Differentiating Equation (
48
) reveals the linear matrix initial value problem

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{Q}}(t)

\displaystyle=\left(\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathbf{H}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\right)\mathbf{Q}(t)

\displaystyle\mathbf{Q}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{W}\;,

(132)

whose unique solution is
\mathbf{Q}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}
. Hereby,
\mathbf{Q}(t)
is constituted of
2N+1
vertically stacked blocks of size
n\times n
, and correspondingly the matrix in round brackets in Equation (
132
) can be separated into
2N+1
row and column blocks each. In this section, we prove Theorem
2
using the same approach as for Theorem
1
in the previous section. Specifically, we show that the stack of blocks defined by the series expression in Equation (
59
), for
j=-N,\dots,N
, satisfies the matrix initial value problem in Equation (
132
).

Proof of Theorem
2
.

We establish absolute convergence of the series expression of Equation (
59
) first. The series

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}}\left\|\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right\|

(133)

is a partial sum of the series in Equation (
124
), which was shown to converge absolutely if Assumption
1
is satisfied. In the truncated case considered here, we can actually drop Assumption
1
: For any multi-index
\mathbf{p}
that appears in Equation (
133
), each entry
p_{k}
must satisfy
\left|p_{k}\right|\leq 2N
, as discussed in Section
3
.
Any nonzero Fourier coefficients
\mathbf{J}_{p}
with
\left|p\right|>2N
do not affect Equation (
133
), so we may, without loss of generality, assume that the Fourier coefficients have finite support
2N
. This ensures absolute convergence of the series, automatically satisfying Assumption
1
.

It remains to show that the right-hand side of Equation (
59
) fulfills the matrix initial value problem given by Equation (
132
). The initial condition is true by definition.
For the differential equation, the approach is analogous to the proof for Theorem
1
, only the summation limits have to be considered carefully now.

The
(jk)
-th block (
k,j=-N,\dots,N
) of the time-periodic matrix
\left(\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathbf{H}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\right)
from the initial value problem of Equation (
132
) is

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathbf{H}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\right)_{jk}=\mathbf{J}_{j-k}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(j-k)\omega t}

(134)

as the matrix
\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}
removes the
\mathrm{i}\omega
-terms in
\mathbf{H}
and pre-multiplication with the diagonal matrix
\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}
scales the rows of
\mathbf{H}
, while post-multiplication with
\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}
scales the columns. For the purpose of this proof, we will call the right-hand side of Equation (
132
)
\widehat{\dot{\mathbf{Q}}}
if the series expression for
\mathbf{Q}
is used, as we do not (yet) know that it actually coincides with
\dot{\mathbf{Q}}
. Evaluating the matrix multiplication block by block with the series expression of Equation (
59
) from Theorem
2
yields

\displaystyle\widehat{\dot{\mathbf{Q}}}_{j}(t):=\left[\left(\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}+\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathbf{H}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\right)\mathbf{Q}(t)\right]_{j}=\sum_{l=-N}^{N}\mathbf{J}_{j-l}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}(j-l)\omega t}\left(\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{l}^{(m)}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right)

(135)

or, after the index shift
\tilde{p}_{1}=j-l
and some reorganization,

\displaystyle\widehat{\dot{\mathbf{Q}}}_{j}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{p}_{1}=j-N}^{j+N}\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{p}_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{p}_{1}\omega t}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\tilde{p}_{1}=j-N}^{j+N}\sum_{[p_{1},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathcal{P}_{j-\tilde{p}_{1}}^{(m)}}\xi_{[p_{1},\dots,p_{m}]}(t)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{p}_{1}\omega t}\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{p}_{1}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;.

(136)

With the relabeling
[p_{1},\dots,p_{m}]:=[\tilde{p}_{2},\dots,\tilde{p}_{m+1}]
and the index shift in
m
by one, the expression

\displaystyle\widehat{\dot{\mathbf{Q}}}_{j}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\tilde{p}_{1}=j-N}^{j+N}\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{p}_{1}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{p}_{1}\omega t}+\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\tilde{p}_{1}=j-N}^{j+N}\sum_{[\tilde{p}_{2},\dots,\tilde{p}_{m}]\in\mathcal{P}_{j-\tilde{p}_{1}}^{(m-1)}}\xi_{[\tilde{p}_{2},\dots,\tilde{p}_{m}]}(t)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\tilde{p}_{1}\omega t}\mathbf{J}_{\tilde{p}_{1}}\mathcal{J}_{[\tilde{p}_{2},\dots,\tilde{p}_{m}]}

(137)

\displaystyle=\sum_{p=j-N}^{j+N}\mathbf{J}_{p}\dot{\xi}_{p}(t)+\sum_{m=2}^{\infty}\sum_{\tilde{p}_{1}=j-N}^{j+N}\sum_{[\tilde{p}_{2},\dots,\tilde{p}_{m}]\in\mathcal{P}_{j-\tilde{p}_{1}}^{(m-1)}}\dot{\xi}_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}}

(138)

follows, where the derivative properties of Lemma
9
were used in the second step. This series is analogous to Equation (
131
) in Section
5.2
, but with nontrivial limits in the sum.
Evaluating the left-hand side of Equation (
132
) by differentiating Equation (
59
) yields

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{Q}}_{j}(t)=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}}\dot{\xi}_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;,

(139)

which is in turn analogous to Equation (
129
). The comparison of Equations (
138
) and (
139
) reveals that the two expressions coincide if the summation sets
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
and
\left\{\mathbf{p}:\left|j-p_{1}\right|\leq N,[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathcal{P}^{(m-1)}_{j-p_{1}}\right\}
are equal. With

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{j}

\displaystyle\iff\left|j-p_{1}\right|\leq N\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\left|j-p_{1}-\sum_{k=1}^{w}p_{k+1}\right|\leq N\text{\penalty\ for all\penalty\ }w=1,\dots,m-1

(140)

\displaystyle\iff p_{1}\in\left\{j-N,\dots,j+N\right\}\text{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathcal{P}^{(m-1)}_{j-p_{1}}\;,

(141)

this is indeed the case and, thu, the series expression of Equation (
59
) solves the matrix differential equation (
132
).
∎

5.4
Proof of Theorem
5
: Series expression for
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)

In this section, we prove Theorem
5
by setting up the series according to Theorem
2
for the subharmonic Fourier series and exploiting the fact that every second Fourier coefficient vanishes.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

Consider a set
\left\{\mathbf{J}_{k}\right\}_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}
of nontrivial (original) Fourier coefficient matrices with the corresponding Fourier series of Equation (
2
) with frequency
\omega
. Consider also the associated subharmonic Fourier series (cf. Equation (
72
)) with frequency
\tilde{\omega}=0.5\omega
and Fourier coefficient matrices
\left\{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}\right\}_{\tilde{k}=-\infty}^{\infty}
. According to Equation (
73
),
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}
is zero for odd
\tilde{k}
.

Let
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)
be constructed according to Equation (
84
), i.e., by the standard Koopman-Hill approach, but applied with even truncation order
\tilde{N}
to the subharmonic Fourier series of Equation (
72
). With Theorem
2
, the
\tilde{j}
-th block of the series expression for
\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}(t)
, expressed in the subharmonic Fourier coefficients
\left\{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}\right\}_{\tilde{k}=-\infty}^{\infty}
, is

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{P}}^{(m)}_{\tilde{j}}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}}\;.

(142)

The
\tilde{}
symbols above
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}
and
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}
indicate that they are evaluated with respect to the subharmonic quantities, i.e.,
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}}
is a product of the
subharmonic
Fourier coefficient matrices
\left\{\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}\right\}_{\tilde{k}=-\infty}^{\infty}
of which every odd one is zero, and
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{j}}^{(m)}
is the integer index set of Equation (
60
), evaluated at
\tilde{j}
and truncation order
\tilde{N}
. This proof will proceed by expressing Equation (
142
) in terms of the original, non-subharmonic Fourier coefficient matrices
\left\{\mathbf{J}_{k}\right\}_{k=-\infty}^{\infty}
and the original integer index sets
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
, evaluated at truncation order
N=0.5\tilde{N}
.

As the Fourier coefficient matrices
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{\tilde{k}}
are zero for odd
\tilde{k}
, products
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{\tilde{\mathbf{p}}}
can only be nonzero if every individual entry of the integer index tuple
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}
is even. This can be ensured by requiring
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}=2\mathbf{p}
with
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
. In other words, we seek an expression for the set of integer indices
\mathbf{p}
such that
2\mathbf{p}
is an element of
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{j}}^{(m)}
. Substituting
\tilde{\mathbf{p}}=2\mathbf{p}
into the condition for the set
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{j}}^{(m)}
yields

\displaystyle\left|\tilde{j}-\sum_{l=1}^{w}2p_{l}\right|\leq\tilde{N}\;,

(143)

or, by substituting
\tilde{N}=2N
and dividing by
2
,

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}-\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}\right|\leq N\;.

(144)

This condition yields exactly the set
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}}
as defined in Equation (
87
). Thus, the summation over
\tilde{\mathcal{P}}_{\tilde{j}}
in Equation (
142
) can equivalently be replaced by
\mathcal{P}_{\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}}
, yielding

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{Q}}_{\tilde{j}}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{\frac{\tilde{j}}{2}}}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{2\mathbf{p}}\;.

(145)

As
\tilde{\mathbf{J}}_{2k}=\mathbf{J}_{k}
implies
\tilde{\mathcal{J}}_{2\mathbf{p}}=\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}
, the proof is complete.
∎

6
Examples

In this section, the error bounds that were derived in Sections
3
and
4
are illustrated and applied to three examples. First, we consider a scalar example with finite support of Fourier coefficients, whose fundamental solution is available in closed form. Afterwards, we discuss how our error bound can offer guaranteed stability determination of the well-known Mathieu equation. Finally, we leverage the Duffing oscillator as an example for the complete workflow including finding a periodic solution and its subsequent stability analysis.

6.1
Scalar example

As a first simple example, consider the scalar linear time-periodic differential equation

\displaystyle\dot{y}=J(t)y=(\beta+2\gamma\cos t)y

(146)

with
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.
As the differential equation is scalar and linear, solutions are available in closed form. In particular, the fundamental solution matrix (here scalar), i.e., the solution initialized at
y(0)=1
, is given by

\displaystyle\phi(t,\beta,\gamma)=\mathrm{e}^{(\beta t+2\gamma\sin t)}\;.

(147)

Using this closed-form solution, the actual error of the direct Koopman-Hill approximation with a given
N
can be compared to the bound of Theorem
4
.
The Fourier coefficients of the system matrix
J(t)
(here scalar) can be immediately read off:
J_{0}=\beta
,
J_{1}=J_{-1}=\gamma
. All other Fourier coefficients are zero. Due to the finite support of the Fourier coefficients, the exponential decay condition of Assumption
1
is satisfied for arbitrary
b>0
and the error bounds of Theorems
4
and
7
hold for all
b>\ln 2
. Nonetheless, the choice
b
does influence the numerical value of the error bound.
Writing
b(\varepsilon)=\ln 2-\ln(\varepsilon)
with
\varepsilon\in(0,1)
, such that
b(\varepsilon)
approaches
\ln 2
as
\varepsilon\rightarrow 1
, the Koopman-Hill error bound in Theorem
4
simplifies to

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{E}(T)\right|\leq\varepsilon^{N}\left(\exp\left(4at\right)-1\right)\;.

(148)

The smaller
\varepsilon
, or equivalently the steeper the exponential decay
b
, the faster the error converges to zero. However, the lowest possible value for
a
increases when
b
is increased. For a given
b(\varepsilon)
, the lowest possible value for
a
in the scalar example with finite support considered here is
a(\varepsilon)=\max\left\{\beta,\frac{2\gamma}{\varepsilon}\right\}
. In the first case
a=\beta
, the exponentially decaying bound
a\mathrm{e}^{-b\left|k\right|}
on the Fourier coefficients is tight on the zero-th harmonic and overshoots the first harmonic, and vice versa for the other case.

Figure
7
shows the error bound of Equation (
148
) for various values of
\varepsilon
and the corresponding best values of
a(\varepsilon)
. The error bound reaches its minimum at a certain optimal value, indicated by a black cross.
If the Fourier coefficient
\gamma
of the first harmonic is dominant over
\beta
(see Figure
7(a)
), the optimal decay is tight at the first harmonic with
a(\varepsilon)=\frac{2\gamma}{\varepsilon}
and the minimum occurs approximately at
\varepsilon^{*}=\frac{8t\gamma}{N}
with
a(\varepsilon^{*})=\frac{N}{4t}
. For the parameters used in this example, truncation orders
N
between 113 and 142 guarantee a sufficiently small error.

However, if the zero-th harmonic coefficient
\beta
is large enough that
\beta>\frac{N}{4t}
,
a(\varepsilon^{*})=\frac{N}{4t}
is not a valid decay parameter. In this case, the error bound is minimized for
\varepsilon^{**}=\frac{2\gamma}{\beta}
, where the exponential decay is tight on both the zero-th and the first harmonic. This is visible in Figure (
7(b)
) where, for smaller
N
, the minimum is located at the kink, where the expression for
a(\varepsilon)
switches. Substituting these optimal values into the error bound of Equation (
148
), the best possible error bound over all choices of the exponential decay is, for the considered dynamical system with finitely supported Fourier coefficients,

\displaystyle E^{*}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\left(\frac{8\gamma t}{N}\right)^{N}\left(\mathrm{e}^{N}-1\right)&\text{if }\beta<\frac{N}{4t}\\
\left(\frac{2\gamma}{\beta}\right)^{N}\left(\mathrm{e}^{4\beta t}-1\right)&\text{otherwise }\;.\end{cases}

(149)

(a)

\beta=0.01
,
\gamma=0.8
. First harmonic
\gamma
dominates.

(b)

\beta=5
,
\gamma=0.8
. Zeroth harmonic
\beta
dominates.

Figure 7
:
Error bound of Equation (
148
) over decay parameter
\varepsilon
for
\gamma=0.8
,
t=6.5
and two values of
\beta
.

Equation (
149
) can numerically be solved for
N
to find the optimal truncation order guaranteed to satisfy a certain error bound. Figure
8(a)
illustrates this truncation order
N^{*}
across a range of desired errors for
\gamma=0.8
. In addition, Figure
8(a)
shows the value of
N
that was needed to approximate the fundamental solution to this accuracy numerically. Figure
8(b)
shows the truncation order
N^{*}
needed to ensure an error smaller than
10^{-6}
for various values of
\gamma
, together with its numerical equivalent. This comparison is also made for the subharmonic approach.

(a)

N^{*}
and
N_{\mathrm{num}}
over
E^{*}
for
\gamma=0.1
.

(b)

N^{*}
and
N_{\mathrm{num}}
over
\gamma
for
E^{*}=10^{-6}
.

Figure 8
:
Guaranteed (
N^{*}
, solid) and actual (
N_{\mathrm{num}}
, dashed) truncation order needed to achieve an error smaller than
E^{*}
in Equation (
149
) with
\beta=0.01,t=6.5
for direct (black) and subharmonic (gray) approach.

It is immediately evident that the derived error bounds become rather conservative as
\gamma
increases. While both the numerical and the guaranteed error grow exponentially with
\gamma
, even for this simple example, the required truncation order
N^{*}
to guarantee sufficiently small errors reaches
N^{*}=100
, while the truncation order that is actually needed remains around
N=10
. This discrepancy arises from the conservative bounding steps employed throughout this work to enable closed-form expressions of the series. Nevertheless, the subharmonic approach consistently requires approximately half the truncation order of the direct approach, as predicted by the error bound.

6.2
Mathieu equation

The next considered example is the well-known Mathieu equation, given by

\displaystyle\ddot{x}+(\delta+\epsilon\cos\omega t)x=0\;.

(150)

The Mathieu equation is a archetypical example of a linear time-periodic system and many applications in physics and engineering can be modeled by it, such as the interaction of gears, vibrations of membranes or the parametric roll of ships. See
[
37
]
for a detailed overview. In first-order form, the Mathieu equation reads

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{y}}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-(\delta+\epsilon\cos\omega t)&0\end{pmatrix}\mathbf{y}=:\mathbf{J}(t)\mathbf{y}\;,

(151)

which is of the form of Equation (
1
). The Fourier coefficients of the system matrix
\mathbf{J}(t)
are
\mathbf{J}_{0}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\delta&0\end{pmatrix}
,
\mathbf{J}_{1}=\mathbf{J}_{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\
-\frac{\epsilon}{2}&0\end{pmatrix}
, and all other Fourier coefficients are zero. Thus, the Fourier coefficients have finite support and the exponential decay condition of Assumption
1
is satisfied for arbitrary
b>0
. As in the previous example, values
a
and
b
that minimize the error bound of Theorem
4
can be identified. With
\beta=\left\|\mathbf{J}_{0}\right\|
and
\gamma=\left\|\mathbf{J}_{1}\right\|=\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, the ideas of the previous section with respect to the optimal error bound of Equation (
149
) carry over.

(a)

\delta=-0.35485
.

(b)

\delta=-0.35490
.

Figure 9
:
Floquet multipliers determined using subharmonic Koopman-Hill approximation and guaranteed regions for true Floquet multipliers for Mathieu equation with
\omega=2
,
\epsilon=2.4
.

In contrast to the scalar example, the stability of the equilibrium of Equation (
151
) is not determined immediately by the monodromy matrix, but by its eigenvalues, the Floquet multipliers. However, the proposed error bounds of Theorems
4
and
7
only provide bounds for perturbations of the monodromy matrix itself.
Bounds for the Floquet multipliers can be obtained with use of the pseudospectrum. When the 2-norm of the difference between the true and approximated monodromy matrix is bounded by
E
, the true Floquet multipliers are contained in the
E
-pseudospectrum
\Lambda_{E}
of the approximated matrix
[
38
]
, given by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{E}(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{approx}})=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:\sigma_{\mathrm{min}}(z\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{approx}})\leq E\right\}\;.

(152)

In words, for all (true) Floquet multipliers
\lambda
, the minimal singular value of
\lambda\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathrm{approx}}
must be smaller than
E
.

Figure
9
illustrates the Floquet multipliers determined using the subharmonic Koopman-Hill approximation for two values of
\delta
that are close to each other. For
\delta=-0.35485
(Figure
9(a)
), the Floquet multipliers are real with one lying outside the unit circle. For
\delta=-0.35490
(Figure
9(b)
), the Floquet multipliers are both complex with
\left|\lambda_{1}\right|=\left|\lambda_{2}\right|=1
. The Floquet multipliers meet at
-1
when
\delta
is varied from the first to the latter value, inducing a stability change. The fundamental solution matrix is not well-conditioned around stability changes, where two Floquet multipliers coincide and the eigenspace deflates.

For the considered values
N\in\left\{44,45,46\right\}
, the approximated Floquet multipliers are identical up to numerical precision. However, the error bound of Equation (
149
) changes, leading to different pseudospectra. It is well known that the Floquet multipliers of the Mathieu equation either lie on the unit circle (stable case) or on the real axis (unstable case)
[
37
]
. For
N=44
and
N=45
, the pseudospectra are large enough that they contain segments of the unit circle as well as segments of the real axis, so correct stability assertion could not be guaranteed using the error bound with these truncation orders. However, for
N=46
, the error bound is small enough that the pseudospectra do not intersect both the unit circle and the real axis, allowing for a stability assertion that is guaranteed to be correct.

Figure
10
shows the truncation orders
N
that are required for various statements about the Mathieu equation with
\epsilon=2.4
,
\omega=2
using the subharmonic Koopman-Hill approximation. The truncation orders are shown over a range of values of
a
that traverse several stability changes of the Mathieu equation. The solid black line reports the truncation orders needed to guarantee an error of the fundamental solution matrix smaller than
10^{-6}
, determined using Equation (
149
). The truncation orders needed to numerically achieve this error are shown in dashed black, determined by computing the actual error for increasing truncation orders until the desired accuracy is reached. The truncation orders needed to guarantee a correct stability assertion, shown in solid gray, are determined by increasing
N
until the pseudospectra do not intersect both the unit circle and the real axis. The truncation orders needed to numerically achieve a correct stability assertion are reported in dashed gray. They are determined by increasing
N
until the stability determined from the approximated Floquet multipliers matches that determined from a high-accuracy reference solution.

The truncation orders needed to guarantee or numerically achieve a certain error of the fundamental matrix are constant over the whole range of
\delta
. Consistently with the previous example and with the simplifications of the proof, the error bound is rather conservative, leading to truncation orders that are significantly larger than those needed to numerically achieve the desired accuracy.

The truncation orders needed for correct stability assertion, however, vary over
\delta
. In the unstable region between
\delta\approx-0.3
and
\delta\approx 2
, the correct stability assertion “unstable” is made immediately with truncation order
1
. This, however, is misleading because for such small truncation orders, the numerically determined Floquet multipliers are still complex conjugated, just far away from the unit circle. In the stable regions, the truncation orders needed for correct stability assertion are significantly larger, as the Floquet multipliers are close to the unit circle and a small error can lead to a wrong stability assertion. Around stability changes, the truncation orders needed for correct stability assertion peak, as the fundamental solution matrix is ill-conditioned there. However, even during these peaks, the truncation orders needed to guarantee correct stability assertion are significantly smaller than those needed to guarantee the error
10^{-6}
. This shows that ill-conditioning during stability changes is not an issue for the method.

Figure 10
:
Truncation orders
N
needed to guarantee (solid) or numerically achieve (dashed) error <
10^{-6}
or correct stability assertion for Mathieu equations with
\epsilon=2.4
,
\omega=2
using subharmonic Koopman-HIll approximation. Stability changes of the ODE indicated by vertical lines.

Figure 11
:
Ince-Strutt diagram for Mathieu equation, computed using subharmonic Koopman-Hill approximation with
N=45
. Grayscale color corresponds to maximum magnitude of computed Floquet multipliers, white (
1
) if stable. Red/blue overlay indicates parameter combinations guaranteed to be stable/unstable using the error bound.

Figure
11
shows the so-called Ince-Strutt-diagram, i.e., the stability regions of the Mathieu equation over
\delta
and
\epsilon
. The stability regions determined using the subharmonic Koopman-Hill approximation with truncation order
N=45
are shown. For every pixel, the stability was determined using the maximum magnitude of the computed Floquet multipliers. Afterwards, the unit circle and the real axis were sampled to assert whether they are both contained in the pseudospectrum of the approximate fundamental solution matrix at this point, determined using the error bound of Equation (
149
).
If only one of the two sets is contained, the stability assertion is guaranteed to be correct and the pixel is colored red (guaranteed stable) or blue (guaranteed unstable). If both sets are contained, correct stability assertion is not guaranteed by the error bound and the pixel is colored in grayscale according to the maximum magnitude of the numerically determined Floquet multipliers.

The resulting Ince-Strutt-diagram is in excellent agreement with the known stability regions of the Mathieu equation
[
37
]
. As expected, large values of
\epsilon
increase the error bound, leading to large pseudospectra that contain both the real axis and the unit circle. While the numerical stability assertion is still correct, larger truncation orders
N
could be chosen in these regions to guarantee correct stability insights. Only minor difficulties in guaranteeing stability are observed close to the stability changes, solidifying the observation that ill-conditioning of the fundamental solution matrix is not a primary issue for the method.

Periodic solutions for the two considered configurations of the Duffing oscillator and norm of Fourier coefficient matrices.

In this example, we consider the forced Duffing oscillator to showcase the applicability of the error bound to stability determination of periodic solutions.
The considered forced Duffing oscillator in first-order form and the corresponding system matrix for a perturbation around a solution
\mathbf{x}(t)
are

\displaystyle\dot{\mathbf{x}}=\mathbf{f}(\mathbf{x})=\begin{pmatrix}x_{2}\\
-\alpha x_{1}-\beta x_{1}^{3}-\delta x_{2}+F\cos\omega t\end{pmatrix}\;,

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}(t)=\left.\frac{\partial\mathbf{f}}{\partial\mathbf{x}}\right|_{\mathbf{x}(t)}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\
-\alpha-3\beta x_{1}(t)^{2}&-\delta\end{pmatrix}\;.

(153)

We consider two parameter settings, detailed in Table
1
.

The periodic solutions of the two configurations, depicted in Figure
12
, were determined using the harmonic balance method with
N=45
Fourier coefficients. Configuration 1, where the forcing frequency is well beyond the resonance frequency and the cubic stiffness is low, could be labeled as an almost-linear case where the periodic solution visually coincides with that of the corresponding linear system (
\beta=0
, all other parameters as in Table
1
), cf. Figure
12(a)
. In Configuration 2, where the cubic stiffness is high and the forcing frequency is low, the higher-frequency components of the periodic solution due to the nonlinear effects are clearly visible in Figure
12(b)
.

The exponential decay of the Fourier coefficients is analyzed in Figures
12(c)
and
12(d)
. In contrast to the previous examples, we now do not have finite support of Fourier coefficients. The exponential parameters
a
and
b
were fitted to the norm of the Fourier coefficient matrices, where only Fourier coefficient matrices with a numerically determined norm larger than
10^{-15}
have been taken into account due to finite numerical precision. The resulting parameters for both configurations are reported in Table
1
.

Figure
13
shows in solid lines the truncation orders
N^{*}
that are expected to guarantee an error smaller than
10^{-6}
using the error bound (
65
).
At each time
t
, the fundamental solution matrices obtained using Equation (
49
) with increasing truncation orders
N
were compared against a reference fundamental solution matrix obtained by integrating Equation (
3
) from
0
to
t
using Matlab’s
ode45
numerical integrator with absolute and relative tolerances set to
10^{-10}
.
For every considered time sample
t
, the lowest value of
N
where the norm of the difference between these two fundamental matrices is lower than
10^{-6}
is reported in dashed lines in Figure
13
.

As in the previous example, the error bound is not tight. In particular, the expected truncation orders
N^{*}
increase linearly with
t
, while the numerically determined truncation orders
N_{\mathrm{num}}
do not. This renders the error bound overly conservative for larger
t
, especially if
b
is small.
