7
Conclusion

In this paper, we provided a convergence proof and the explicit error bounds given by Equations (
64
) and (
101
) for the numerical computation of the fundamental solution matrix of an LTP system using two variants of the Koopman-Hill projection method. The proof relies on expressing both the fundamental solution matrix as well as its approximation as series and then comparing the summands. While the series expressions are well-defined for all time-periodic system matrices where the Fourier coefficients decay exponentially, the error bounds and thus the convergence proof are valid for systems where the decay rate is sufficiently large (
b>\ln 2
).

Theorem
4
provides valuable theoretical backing for the use of the Hill matrix in the context of the harmonic balance method.
Up until now, the only available convergence guarantee for any Hill-matrix-based method was in the context of the imaginary-part-based sorting method
[
19
, Prop. 3]
. While applicable to system matrices that are differentiable with piecewise continuous derivative, which is a larger system class than our proof, the proof of
[
19
]
does not come with an explicit error bound and gives no indication at all about how to choose
N
to be sufficiently large in practice.
Thus, the error bound derived in the present work is the most explicit one available for all Hill matrix approaches and provides a helpful justification for the practitioner with an upper bound on the required truncation order
N
.

The examples of Section
6
show that the error bound is conservative and overestimates the required truncation order
N
. This conservatism arises from the bounding steps employed to enable closed-form expressions of the series. Specifically, to upper-bound the number of index tuples with a given
1
-norm in the series for the error,
all
integer index tuples that do not lie in the largest rhombus within
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
(or
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{subh}}^{(m)}
) were counted, irrespective of whether they actually lie inside
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
(resp.
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{\mathrm{subh}}
) or not. The ratio of tuples in this rhombus over all tuples in
\mathcal{P}_{0}^{(m)}
or
\mathcal{P}^{(m)}_{\mathrm{subh}}
goes to zero as
m
grows. Hence, especially for large
m
, our error bound includes significantly more summands than necessary, contributing to overly conservative convergence requirements.
Furthermore, the error bound depends exponentially on the time
t
. However, the numerical results do not indicate any dependence on time at all. Potentially, a tighter bound independent of
t
could be derived by exploiting the periodicity of the scalar factors or finding a better method to count only those scalar factors that actually lie outside the eligible index set.
Additionally, algebraic simplifications like
\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{m}<1
and
\binom{N+m+1}{m}^{-1}<1
in the proof of Lemma
16
or summing from
0
to
N+m
instead of
1
to
m
in the final step of the convergence proof become more conservative as
N
and
m
grow.
For these reasons, we postulate that a similar bound to Equation (
64
), but independent of
t
and applicable whenever Assumption
1
is satisfied, is likely to exist. This would make the result tighter and applicable to all analytic linear periodic differential equations. Further work is needed to derive such a bound.

This paper focused mainly on the error in the fundamental solution matrix, caused by truncation of the Hill matrix. We illustrated in Section
6.2
how the effect of the bound on the Floquet multipliers can be analyzed using the pseudospectrum of the approximation.

In practice, all involved operations are executed with finite machine precision on a computer. The error due to numerical procedures, in particular the error of evaluating the matrix exponential, is not considered in the error bound of Equation (
64
). However, the accurate numerical evaluation of matrix exponentials has been well researched
[
39
,
40
,
41
]
, so the error due to the finite-precision procedures is expected to be negligible compared to the truncation error in practical applications.

Appendix A
Construction of the series expressions of Theorems
1
and
2

The proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
in Section
5
rely on differentiation of the proposed series expressions, verifying that they uniquely solve the associated matrix initial value problems. While this approach is concise and rigorous, it sheds no light on the underlying construction of the series representations in Equations (
56
) and (
59
). To complement the main developments, this section outlines an alternative procedure for deriving these series. Specifically, we consider the Taylor expansions of
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
and
\mathbf{Q}(t)
about
t=0
, substitute the Fourier series representation of
\mathbf{J}(t)
, and employ inductive arguments to reorganize the resulting expressions into the forms given by Equations (
56
) and (
59
). As complete proofs are already provided in Section
5
, we restrict ourselves here to a conceptual overview, assuming absolute convergence and sufficiently large radius of convergence for all series encountered.

A.1
Taylor series for
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)

To write the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
given by the matrix initial value problem of Equation (
3
) as a Taylor series around
0
, all derivatives of
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
and
\mathbf{Q}(t)
are required. The
0
-th and first derivative are immediately clear from the matrix initial value problem (
3
). Further derivatives can be computed using the differential equation and the product rule, utilizing the Fourier series expression of
\mathbf{J}(t)
. This is formalized in the following lemma.

Lemma 10
.

For all natural
l>0
, the
l
-th derivative of the fundamental solution matrix
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
defined by Equation (
8
) is

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}\mathbf{\Phi}(t):=\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)\mathbf{\Phi}(t)

(154)

with

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)=\sum_{m=1}^{l}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{l-m}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\\
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|=l-m\end{subarray}}\left[p_{1},(p_{1}+p_{2}),\dots,(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{k}\right)\omega t}\;.

(155)

Proof.

This lemma can be proven by induction. The base case
n=1
with
\mathbf{J}^{(1)}(t)=\sum_{p\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbf{J}_{p}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}p\omega t}=\mathbf{J}(t)
is immediately clear. For the induction step, differentiation of Equation (
154
) and the use of the IVP reveal

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{(l+1)}}{\mathrm{d}t^{(l+1)}}\mathbf{\Phi}(t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\left(\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)\right)\mathbf{\Phi}(t)+\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)\mathbf{J}(t)\mathbf{\Phi}(t)\;,

(156)

i.e.,
\mathbf{J}^{(l+1)}(t)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)+\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)\mathbf{J}(t)
is certainly a
T
-periodic matrix. Note that
\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)
is generally not the
l
-th derivative of
\mathbf{J}(t)
. Substituting in Equation (
155
) and the Taylor series expansion of
\mathbf{J}(t)
yields after some algebraic manipulation and index shifts

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=1}^{l}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{l-m+1}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\\
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|=l-m+1\\
\alpha_{m}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\left[p_{1},(p_{1}+p_{2}),\dots,(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{k}\right)\omega t}

(157)

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(t)\mathbf{J}(t)

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=2}^{l+1}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{l-m+1}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\\
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|=l-m+1\\
\alpha_{m}=0\end{subarray}}\left[p_{1},(p_{1}+p_{2}),\dots,(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\right)\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{k}\right)\omega t}\;.

(158)

The summation sets in the round brackets of Equations (
157
) and (
158
) are disjunct. As the summation over
\bm{\alpha}
in Equation (
157
) has no valid summands for
m=l+1
and, similarly, there are no valid summands for
\bm{\alpha}
with
m=1
in Equation (
158
), both series over
m
in Equations (
157
) and (
158
) can be extended to sum from
m=1
to
m=l+1
. Together, Equations (
157
) and (
158
) constitute the complete summation within the round brackets of Equation (
155
).
∎

With Lemma
10
, the Taylor series of
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)
is given by
\mathbf{\Phi}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{l=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{J}^{(l)}(0)\frac{t^{l}}{l!}
. Substituting in Equation (
155
) and changing the order of the
m
- and
l
-summations yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{\Phi}(t)=\mathbf{I}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}}\sum_{l=m}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\\
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|=l-m\end{subarray}}\frac{t^{l}}{l!}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{l-m}\left[p_{1},(p_{1}+p_{2}),\dots,(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\;.

(159)

Noting that every
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
fulfills
l=m+\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|
for exactly one value of
l\geq m
, the
l
-summation can be eliminated and the desired Equation (
56
) results.

A.2
Taylor series for
\mathbf{Q}(t)

To express
\mathbf{Q}(t)
as a Taylor series around
0
, again all derivatives are needed. Analogous to Lemma
10
, the following lemma provides a series expression for derivatives of
\mathbf{Q}(t)
.

Lemma 11
.

For
l\in\mathbb{N}
, the
l
-th derivative of
\mathbf{Q}(t)
as defined in Equation (
48
) is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l}}\mathbf{Q}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\mathbf{A}^{(l)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}\;.

(160)

The matrix
\mathbf{A}^{(l)}\in\mathbb{C}^{n(2N+1)\times n(2N+1)}
is constant and consists of blocks
\mathbf{A}^{(l)}_{jk}
,
j,k=-N,\dots,N
, of size
n\times n
. For
l=0
, it holds that
\mathbf{A}^{(0)}=\mathbf{I}
and for
l\geq 1

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{(l)}_{jk}=\sum_{m=1}^{l}(\mathrm{i}\omega)^{l-m}\sum_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{A}_{jk}^{(m)}}\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}\\
\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|=l-m\end{subarray}}\left[p_{1},(p_{1}+p_{2}),\dots,(p_{1}+\dots+p_{m})\right]^{\bm{\alpha}}\mathcal{J}_{\mathbf{p}}\right)\;.

(161)

The index set
\mathcal{A}_{jk}^{(m)}
is a subset of
\mathbb{Z}^{m}
that is independent of
l
and is given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{jk}^{(m)}=\left\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:k=j-\sum_{i=1}^{m}p_{i}\textrm{\penalty\ and\penalty\ }\left|j-\sum_{i=1}^{w}p_{i}\right|\leq N\textrm{\penalty\ for\penalty\ }w=1,\dots,m\right\}\;.

(162)

The integer index set
\mathcal{P}_{j}^{(m)}
which plays an important role in the main part of this work is given by the union of
\mathcal{A}^{(m)}_{jk}
over
k=-N,\dots,N
.

Proof.

The proof is similar to the one of Lemma
10
. The induction base case
\mathbf{A}^{(0)}=\mathbf{I}
is true by definition. By differentiating Equation (
160
), we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}^{l+1}}{\mathrm{d}t^{l+1}}\mathbf{Q}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}t}\left(\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}\mathbf{A}^{(l)}+\mathbf{A}^{(l)}\mathbf{H}\right)\mathrm{e}^{\mathbf{H}t}\mathbf{W}\;,

(163)

which provides a recursive relationship for
\mathbf{A}^{(l)}
. A slight reformulation yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{(l+1)}=\mathrm{i}\omega\left(\mathbf{D}\mathbf{A}^{(l)}-\mathbf{A}^{(l)}\mathbf{D}\right)+\mathbf{A}^{(l)}\left(\mathbf{H}+\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}\right)\;.

(164)

In the first summand of Equation (
164
), the
(jk)
-th
n\times n
block of
\mathbf{A}^{(l)}
is scaled by
\mathrm{i}\omega(j-k)
. In the second summand, the addition of
\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}
removes the corresponding terms on the diagonal of
\mathbf{H}
(cf. Equation (
2.2
)), such that the matrix that is multiplied to
\mathbf{A}^{(l)}
is purely block-Toeplitz.

For
l=1
, Equation (
164
) becomes
\mathbf{A}^{(1)}=\mathbf{H}+\mathrm{i}\omega\mathbf{D}
, whose
(jk)
-th block is the Fourier coefficient
\mathbf{J}_{j-k}
(cf. Equation (
28
)). With
l=1
, the summation over
m
in Equation (
161
) only has one summand. It can be verified easily that the corresponding index set
\mathcal{A}_{jk}^{(1)}
contains only
p=k-j
and thus Equation (
161
) becomes
\mathbf{J}_{j-k}
for
l=1
.
For
l>1
, it can be shown by an induction procedure, which is analogous to the one in
A.1
but significantly more tedious due to the sets
\mathcal{A}_{jk}^{(m)}
, that the blocks
\mathbf{A}_{jk}
are indeed given by Equation (
161
).
∎

With Lemma
11
, the
l
-th derivative of
\mathbf{Q}
at
0
is
\mathbf{A}^{(l)}\mathbf{W}
. As
\mathbf{W}
consists of identity matrices, the
j
-th block of
\mathbf{A}^{(l)}\mathbf{W}
can be written as
\sum_{k=-N}^{N}\mathbf{A}_{jk}^{(l)}
. Analogously as in
A.1
, establishing a Taylor series using all these derivatives leads to Equation (
59
).

Appendix B
Periodicity of the scalar factor

An observation that can be drawn from Figure
2
is that the scalar factor
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
has periodic components, possibly multiplied by a monomial in
t
. We show here that this monomial vanishes for many values of
\mathbf{p}
, making
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
in these cases
T
-periodic and bounded.

Theorem 12

(sufficient criterion for
T
-periodicity of
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
)
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
and consider an integer index tuple
\mathbf{p}=[p_{1},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
which fulfills
\sum_{l=v}^{w}p_{l}\neq 0
for all
v,w=1,\dots,m
. Then, the function
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
is
T
-periodic and has a Fourier series
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=:\sum_{k=-\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}^{\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}\xi_{\mathbf{p}}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}k\omega t}
with finite support. In particular, the
k
-th Fourier coefficient
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}^{(k)}
can only be nonzero if
k=0
or if there exists a
w\in\left\{1,\dots,m\right\}
such that
k=\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}
.

Proof.

We prove this by induction.

Base case
m=1

Integrating the first statement of Lemma
9
for an arbitrary
p\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
with
\xi_{p}(0)=0
yields

\displaystyle\xi_{p}(t)=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega p}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega pt}-1\right)\;.

(165)

In particular,
\xi_{p}(t)
is
T
-periodic and only the
0
-th and
p
-th Fourier coefficients are nonzero.

Induction assumption

Let
m\geq 2
. Consider an integer index tuple
\mathbf{p}=[p_{1},p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}
fulfilling the conditions of the theorem. The tuple
[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]\in\mathbb{Z}^{m-1}
fulfills the conditions of the theorem as well. The induction assumption is that
\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}(t)
is
T
-periodic and its Fourier coefficients
\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}^{(k)}
are only nonzero if
k=0
or if there exists a
w
such that
k=\sum_{l=2}^{w}p_{l}
.

Induction step

Using the induction assumption, the second statement of Lemma
9
, and the initial condition
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(0)=0
,
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
can be expressed by

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}(\tau)\,\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega p_{1}\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{k=-\left|\mathbf{p}\right|+\left|p_{1}\right|}^{\left|\mathbf{p}\right|-\left|p_{1}\right|}\xi_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}^{(k)}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(k+p_{1})\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau\;.

(166)

This integral can be evaluated summand by summand. For
k=-p_{1}
, the exponential term in the integrand becomes
1
, yielding the non-periodic, linear term

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\xi^{(-p_{1})}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}\mathrm{d}\tau=t\,\xi^{(-p_{1})}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}\;.

(167)

Assume now that
\xi^{(-p_{1})}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}
is nonzero. As
p_{1}=\sum_{l=1}^{1}p_{l}\neq 0
, by the induction assumption there must exist a
w
such that
-p_{1}=\sum_{l=2}^{w}p_{l}
. But this is prohibited by construction of
\mathbf{p}
as it would imply
\sum_{l=1}^{w}p_{l}=0
. We conclude that
\xi^{(-p_{1})}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}
must be zero and
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}(t)
does not have a non-periodic term of the form of Equation (
167
).

For
k\neq-p_{1}
, the exponential term in the integrand does not disappear and we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\xi^{(k)}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(k+p_{1})\tau}\mathrm{d}\tau=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega(k+p_{1})}\xi^{(k)}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\omega(k+p_{1})t}-1\right)\;.

(168)

As Equation (
167
) vanishes and all other summands are of the form of Equation (
168
), which is
T
-periodic,
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
is again
T
-periodic. In particular, Equation (
168
) allows to read off the Fourier coefficients of
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}
:

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathbf{p}}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=-\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}^{\left|\mathbf{p}\right|}\frac{-1}{\mathrm{i}\omega\left(k+p_{1}\right)}\xi^{(k)}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}

(169a)

\displaystyle\xi_{\mathbf{p}}^{(k)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}\omega k}\xi^{(k-p_{1})}_{[p_{2},\dots,p_{m}]}

\displaystyle k

\displaystyle\neq 0\;.

(169b)

By the induction assumption,
\xi_{\mathbf{p}}^{(k)}
for
k\neq 0
can only be nonzero if there is a
w
such that
k-p_{1}=\sum_{l=2}^{w}p_{l}
, which completes the proof.

∎

Appendix C
Combinatorics

This section and the following one collect some particular equivalences that are needed to handle the various series expressions occurring in this work. Some of these results are well-known and revisited here for the sake of completeness, while others are rather specific. We begin by stating a well-known result on the number of multi-indices with a given 1-norm.

Lemma 13

(Stars and bars
[
42
]
)
.

i)

The set
\left\{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}^{m}:\left|\bm{\alpha}\right|=M\right\}
has exactly
\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}
elements.

ii)

The set
\left\{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}^{m}:\left|\mathbf{p}\right|=M\right\}
has fewer than
2^{m}\binom{M+m-1}{m-1}
elements.

Proof.

Proposition
i)
is a classical combinatorics result that can be found, e.g., in
[
42
]
. For any nonnegative tuple
\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{N}
there are at most
2^{m}
tuples
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{Z}
which fulfill
\left|p_{k}\right|=\alpha_{k}
for all
k=1,\dots,m
as every entry can be either positive or negative (yielding two independent options for each of the
m
entries), except if it is zero. This immediately proves Proposition
ii)
.
∎

Next, we provide here a variant of Vandermonde’s identity
[
42
]
with the summation index in the upper entries, which enables a follow-up result about summation over multiple indices.

Lemma 14
.

For arbitrary
n,M,P\in\mathbb{N}
, the following expression holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha=0}^{M}\binom{\alpha+n}{n}\binom{M+P-\alpha}{M-\alpha}=\binom{M+P+n+1}{M}\;.

(170)

Proof.

This proof follows a classical counting argument. Consider the set
\mathcal{D}:=\left\{1,\dots,M+P+n+1\right\}
, which contains all strictly positive natural numbers up to
{M+P+n+1}
. The number of subsets of the form
\mathcal{A}:=\left\{a_{1},\dots,a_{P+n+1}\right\}\subset\mathcal{D}
with
P+n+1
unique elements
a_{1}<a_{2}<\dots<a_{P+n+1}
is
\binom{M+P+n+1}{P+n+1}=\binom{M+P+n+1}{M}
, which is the right-hand side of Equation (
170
).

For the left-hand side, we construct another way to count these subsets. The
(n+1)
-th element
a_{n+1}
must have a value between
n+1
(implying
a_{k}=k
for the
n
elements
a_{k}
with
k<n+1
) and
n+1+M
(implying
a_{k}=M+k
for the
P
elements with
k>n+1
). Suppose that
a_{n+1}=n+1+\alpha
for some
\alpha\in\left\{0,\dots,M\right\}
. As the elements before
a_{n+1}
must have smaller value and the elements after must have larger value, these subsets of
\mathcal{A}
must fulfill

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{-}

\displaystyle:=\left\{a_{1},\dots,a_{n}\right\}\subset\left\{1,\dots,n+\alpha\right\}

(171)

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{+}

\displaystyle:=\left\{a_{n+2},\dots,a_{n+P+1}\right\}\subset\left\{n+\alpha+2,\dots,M+P+n+1\right\}\;.

(172)

Hence, for
\mathcal{A}_{-}
we choose
n
out of
n+\alpha
values, while for
\mathcal{A}_{+}
we choose
P
out of
M+P-\alpha
values. In summary, for every fixed
\alpha
there are
\binom{n+\alpha}{n}\binom{M+P-\alpha}{P}=\binom{n+\alpha}{n}\binom{M+P-\alpha}{M-\alpha}
subsets
\mathcal{A}
where
a_{n+1}=n+1+\alpha
, and summing over all possible values of
\alpha
completes the proof.
∎

This lemma enables a follow-up statement that deals with multiple sums.

Lemma 15
.

For arbitrary
M\in\mathbb{N}
,
m\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\left\{0\right\}
and
\mathbf{n}=[n_{1},\dots,n_{m}]\in\mathbb{N}^{m}
, the following expression given by
m
finite-length sums holds:

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}=0}^{M}\sum_{\alpha_{2}=0}^{M-\alpha_{1}}\dots\sum_{\alpha_{m}=0}^{M-\alpha_{1}-\dots-\alpha_{m-1}}\binom{\alpha_{1}+n_{1}}{n_{1}}\dots\binom{\alpha_{m}+n_{m}}{n_{m}}=\binom{m+M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}{M}\;.

(173)

Proof.

We prove this statement using Lemma
14
and induction.

Base case
m=1

The base case is covered immediately by Lemma
14
with
P=0
.

Induction assumption

Assume that Lemma
15
holds for the case
m-1
, i.e. for
m-1
summation symbols. By increasing the index of all
\alpha_{i}
and
n_{i}
by 1 and replacing
M
by
M-\alpha_{1}
for arbitrary
\alpha_{1}
and
M\geq\alpha_{1}
, we can rewrite the induction assumption as

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{2}=0}^{M-\alpha_{1}}\dots\sum_{\alpha_{m}=0}^{M-\alpha_{1}-\dots-\alpha_{m-1}}\binom{\alpha_{2}+n_{2}}{n_{2}}\dots\binom{\alpha_{m}+n_{m}}{n_{m}}=\binom{(m-1)+(M-\alpha_{1})+n_{2}+\dots+n_{m}}{M-\alpha_{1}}\;.

(174)

Induction step

In Equation (
173
), the first binomial coefficient depends only on
\alpha_{1}
and can be pulled outside of the sums over
\alpha_{2},\dots,\alpha_{m}
. Afterwards, the induction assumption Equation (
174
) can be identified in the inner sums and the statement left to prove is

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha_{1}=0}^{M}\binom{\alpha_{1}+n_{1}}{n_{1}}\binom{m-1+M-\alpha_{1}+n_{2}+\dots+n_{m}}{M-\alpha_{1}}=\binom{m+M+\left|\mathbf{n}\right|}{M}\;.

(175)

As this statement is covered by Lemma
14
with
P=m-1+n_{2}+\dots+n_{m}
, the proof is complete.
∎

Appendix D
Taylor series of
(1-x)^{-(1+k)}

The following power series plays a central role in the convergence proof.

Lemma 16
.

For arbitrary
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
q\in\mathbb{R}
with
q\geq 0
, the power series

\displaystyle\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\binom{M+k}{k}M^{q}x^{M}

(176)

converges absolutely if
\left|x\right|<1
. In the case
q=0
, the series has the closed-form expression

\displaystyle\sum_{M=0}^{\infty}\binom{M+k}{k}x^{M}=(1-x)^{-(1+k)}=:g(x)\;.

(177)

The Taylor remainder for a Taylor polynomial of degree
N
of this series is given by

\displaystyle R_{N}(x):=\sum_{M=N+1}^{\infty}\binom{M+k}{k}x^{M}=x^{N}\left(\sum_{m=0}^{k}\binom{N+k+1}{N+m+1}\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{m+1}\right)\;.

(178)

Proof.

Denote summands of Equation (
176
) by
a_{M}
. The quotient criterion yields

\displaystyle\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\left|\frac{a_{M+1}}{a_{M}}\right|=\lim_{M\rightarrow\infty}\frac{M+k+1}{M+k}\left(\frac{M+1}{M}\right)^{q}\left|x\right|=\left|x\right|\;,

(179)

proving absolute convergence for
\left|x\right|<1
and
q\geq 0
.
For the special case
q=0
, a straightforward induction procedure shows that the
M
-th derivative of

g(x)=(1-x)^{-(1+k)}
is

\displaystyle g^{(M)}(x):=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{M}}{\mathrm{d}x^{M}}(1-x)^{-(1+k)}=\frac{(M+k)!}{k!}(1-x)^{-(M+k+1)}

\displaystyle\text{\penalty\ for all\penalty\ }M\in\mathbb{N}\;.

(180)

Evaluated at zero, these derivatives define the Taylor formula

\displaystyle g(x)=(1-x)^{-(k+1)}=\sum_{M=0}^{N}\frac{(M+k)!}{k!}\frac{x^{M}}{M!}+R_{N}(x)\;,

(181)

where
N\in\mathbb{N}
is the maximum degree of the Taylor polynomial and
R_{N}(x)
is the remainder.

The Taylor remainder in integral form
[
43
]
is given by

\displaystyle R_{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x}\frac{(x-\tau)^{N}}{N!}g^{(N+1)}(\tau)\mathrm{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{x}\frac{(x-\tau)^{N}}{N!}\frac{(N+1+k)!}{k!}(1-\tau)^{-(2+k+N)}\mathrm{d}\tau\;.

(182)

The substitution
u=(1-\tau)^{-1}
of the integration variable simplifies this integral to

\displaystyle R_{N}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{(1-x)^{-1}}\frac{u^{k}}{k!}\,\frac{(N+k+1)!}{N!}\,(1-(1-x)u)^{N}\mathrm{d}u\;,

(183)

which is an integral over a polynomial in
u
. We evaluate this integral using
(k+1)
-times repeated integration by parts. The integrand of Equation (
183
) is given by the product of the functions

\displaystyle v^{(0)}(u)

\displaystyle:=\frac{u^{k}}{k!}

(184)

\displaystyle W^{(0)}(u)

\displaystyle:=\frac{(N+k+1)!}{N!}(1-(1-x)u)^{N}\;.

(185)

For
v
, we can immediately compute its
m
-th derivatives

\displaystyle v^{(m)}(u):=\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}v^{(0)}(u)=\frac{u^{k-m}}{(k-m)!}

\displaystyle m=0,\dots,k

(186)

and the
(k+1)
-th derivative is zero. The function
W^{(0)}
has the
m
-th antiderivative

\displaystyle W^{(m)}(u):=(x-1)^{-m}\,\frac{(N+k+1)!}{(N+m)!}\,(1-(1-x)u)^{N+m}

(187)

such that
\frac{\mathrm{d}^{m}}{\mathrm{d}u^{m}}W^{(m)}(u)=W^{(0)}(u)
for
m=0,\dots,k+1
. Performing
k+1
times the integration by parts on Equation (
183
), each time using the next derivative of
v
and the next antiderivative of
W
, yields the formula

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{(1-x)^{-1}}v^{(0)}W^{(0)}\mathrm{d}u=\sum_{m=0}^{k}(-1)^{m}\left[W^{(m+1)}v^{(m)}\right]_{1}^{(1-x)^{-1}}\!\!\!+(-1)^{k+1}\int_{1}^{(1-x)^{-1}}W^{(k+1)}v^{(k+1)}\mathrm{d}u\;,

(188)

where the dependence on
u
was omitted for the sake of brevity. The integral on the right-hand side of Equation (
188
) vanishes together with
v^{(k+1)}
, and each summand of the remaining boundary terms can be evaluated individually to

\displaystyle(-1)^{m}\left[W^{(m+1)}v^{(m)}\right]_{1}^{(1\!-\!x)^{-1}}\!\!\!

\displaystyle=\left[-(1\!-\!x)^{-(m+1)}\tfrac{(N+k+1)!}{(N+m+1)!(k-m)!}(1\!-\!(1\!-\!x)u)^{N+m+1}u^{k-m}\right]_{1}^{(1-x)^{-1}}

\displaystyle=x^{N}\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{m+1}\binom{N+k+1}{N+m+1}\;.

(189)

Substituting Equation (
189
) into Equation (
188
) yields the desired expression, Equation (
178
), for the remainder.

To show that the remainder converges to zero as
N\rightarrow\infty
, we will proceed to bound the expression (
178
) from above. To lighten notation, we assume w.l.o.g that
x>0
(otherwise, replace
x
by
\left|x\right|
and
R_{N}(x)
by
\left|R_{N}(x)\right|
in the developments below). From the factorial expressions it is easy to see that

\displaystyle\binom{N+k+1}{N+m+1}=\binom{N+k+1}{N+1}\binom{k}{m}\binom{N+m+1}{m}^{-1}\;.

(190)

Noting that
\binom{N+m+1}{m}^{-1}\leq 1
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
, we substitute Equation (
190
) into the remainder (
178
) and use the binomial theorem to obtain

\displaystyle R_{N}(x)

\displaystyle\leq x^{N}\frac{x}{1-x}\binom{N+k+1}{N+1}\sum_{m=0}^{k}\binom{k}{m}\left(\frac{x}{1-x}\right)^{m}1^{k-m}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{N+1}}{(1-x)}\binom{N+k+1}{N+1}\left(\frac{x}{1-x}+1\right)^{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{x^{N+1}}{(1-x)^{k+1}}\binom{N+k+1}{N+1}\;.

(191)

Finally, using the bound
\binom{N+1+k}{k}\leq(N+1)^{k+1}
, we find for any fixed
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle 0\leq\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}R_{N}(x)\leq\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}x^{N+1}\left(\frac{N+1}{1-x}\right)^{k+1}=0\;,

(192)

completing the proof.
∎
